We evaluate the effectiveness of SAEs when applied to representations from GPT-2 Small and Gemma 2B, examining their capacity to highlight buggy behaviour without fine-tuning the underlying LLMs. We found that SAE-derived features enable bug detection with an F1 score of up to 89%, consistently outperforming fine-tuned transformer encoder baselines. Our work provides the first empirical evidence that SAEs can be used to detect software bugs directly from the internal representations of pretrained LLMs, without any fine-tuning or task-specific supervision.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the use of Sparse Autoencoders (SAEs) for Java function bug detection, leveraging internal representations from Large Language Models (LLMs) without fine-tuning. It investigates whether SAEs can serve as a lightweight, interpretable alternative to traditional methods. The authors apply SAEs to GPT-2 Small and Gemma 2B, examining their capacity to highlight buggy behavior. They find that features derived from SAEs enable bug detection with an F1 score of up to 89%, outperforming fine-tuned transformer encoder baselines. The work provides empirical evidence that SAEs can detect software bugs directly from LLM internal representations, without fine-tuning or task-specific supervision.  It showcases the potential of integrating interpretability tools into security-critical workflows.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the application of mechanistic interpretability techniques, specifically SAEs, to the domain of software bug detection within Java functions.  While SAEs have been used in other contexts (e.g., natural language processing, feature extraction), their application to the *internal activations* of LLMs for *software security* is a novel contribution. The layer-wise analysis of model representations across multiple models (GPT-2 Small and Gemma 2B) and the demonstration of lightweight classification using SAE-extracted features also contribute to the novelty.  The paper differentiates itself by *not* relying on fine-tuning, a common approach in this area, instead focusing on repurposing pre-trained LLM representations.

**Significance:**  The significance stems from several factors:

*   **Bridging Interpretability and Security:** It directly addresses the need for interpretable AI systems in security-sensitive applications. The complexity and opacity of LLMs pose challenges for their deployment in such contexts, and this work provides a potential solution.
*   **Lightweight and Scalable Approach:** The proposed method is more lightweight and scalable compared to fine-tuning approaches, as it leverages pre-trained LLMs and simple classifiers.
*   **Practical Implications:**  The results suggest that SAEs can be used to surface actionable signals for vulnerability detection, potentially aiding developers in identifying and fixing bugs more effectively.
*   **New Research Direction:** This work opens up new avenues for research at the intersection of AI safety, software engineering, and interpretability. It encourages further exploration of how mechanistic interpretability tools can be used to address real-world security challenges.
*   **Comparison against baselines:** Comparing the model against SOTA approaches like graphcodeBERT and other transformer-based models adds significantly to the credibility and importance of the research.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Statement:** The paper clearly defines the problem of vulnerability detection and the challenges associated with traditional methods and LLMs.
*   **Well-Defined Methodology:** The methodology is well-structured and clearly explained, including the data preprocessing, SAE training, feature selection, and classification steps.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The evaluation is comprehensive, with experiments conducted across multiple datasets (Defects4J, HumanEval, Github-Bug Java) and models (GPT-2 Small, Gemma 2B).  The layer-wise analysis provides valuable insights into model behavior.
*   **Strong Results:** The results are compelling, demonstrating that SAE-extracted features can achieve high F1 scores in bug detection, outperforming fine-tuned baselines. The transferability analysis adds further value.
*   **Interpretability Focus:** The paper maintains a strong focus on interpretability, emphasizing the use of SAEs to surface actionable features and understand model decision-making processes.
*   **Discussion of Limitations:** The authors acknowledge the limitations of their work, including potential interpretability caveats related to SAEs, the cost of SAE pretraining, and the limited scope of the classification tasks.  This contributes to the credibility of the research.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Interpretability Concerns:** As acknowledged in the paper's "Threats to Validity" section, SAEs may produce seemingly interpretable features even in untrained models, challenging the assumption that extracted features always reflect meaningful computational circuits. While predictive, the features' true semantic grounding warrants further investigation.  The extent to which the "interpretability" is genuinely revealing the *cause* of buggy behavior, rather than simply correlating with it, needs more rigorous scrutiny.
*   **Generalizability:** Although the experiments are conducted on multiple datasets, the focus is limited to Java functions. The generalizability of the results to other programming languages and more complex code structures remains an open question. While Github Bug Java is a real world data set, there may be selection bias in the type of bugs discovered.
*   **SAE Training Cost:** The reliance on pre-trained SAEs, while avoiding the need for LLM fine-tuning, still introduces a significant overhead in terms of computational resources and expertise required to train high-quality SAEs, particularly for large models. This could limit the accessibility of the approach.
*  **Token-Level analysis:** The token level analysis is good, but more examples illustrating the utility of the SAE features would strengthen the case.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper has the potential to influence the field by:

*   Promoting the use of mechanistic interpretability tools in software security.
*   Providing a practical and scalable approach to bug detection using SAEs.
*   Encouraging further research on the use of sparse representations for security applications.
*   Highlighting the importance of combining AI-driven techniques with traditional code analysis tools.

**Justification for the Score:**

I am assigning a score of **7.5** to this paper.

**Rationale:** The paper represents a *significant and novel contribution* to the intersection of AI safety, software engineering, and interpretability. The application of SAEs to bug detection, the strong empirical results, and the focus on interpretability make it a valuable contribution. The comparisons against SOTA add to the credibility.

However, there are aspects preventing a higher score:

*   The validity concerns regarding the true "interpretability" of SAE features.
*   The limited scope of the experiments (Java functions only).
*   The reliance on pre-trained SAEs, which introduces a non-negligible training cost.
*   Room for more in depth analysis of the token features.

These weaknesses, while acknowledged by the authors, somewhat limit the immediate impact and generalizability of the work.  Despite these limitations, the paper offers a compelling proof-of-concept and provides a strong foundation for future research in this area, warranting a score above average. The impact on the field and industry will depend on resolving the limitations and generalizing the techniques to a broader set of problems.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 7/10

### Multi-domain Multilingual Sentiment Analysis in Industry: Predicting Aspect-based Opinion Quadruples
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10389v1)
- **Authors**: Benjamin White, Anastasia Shimorina
- **Abstract**: This paper explores the design of an aspect-based sentiment analysis system using large language models (LLMs) for real-world use. We focus on quadruple opinion extraction -- identifying aspect categories, sentiment polarity, targets, and opinion expressions from text data across different domains and languages. Using internal datasets, we investigate whether a single fine-tuned model can effectively handle multiple domain-specific taxonomies simultaneously. We demonstrate that a combined multi-domain model achieves performance comparable to specialized single-domain models while reducing operational complexity. We also share lessons learned for handling non-extractive predictions and evaluating various failure modes when developing LLM-based systems for structured prediction tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper presents an industrial application of Aspect-Based Sentiment Analysis (ABSA) using Large Language Models (LLMs). The authors address the challenge of managing multiple domain-specific taxonomies in their internal OpinionTool, which analyzes user reviews and employee surveys.  They explore fine-tuning LLMs for quadruple opinion extraction (aspect category, sentiment polarity, target, and opinion expression) across different domains and languages.  The key investigation compares single-domain fine-tuned models against a multi-domain model trained on the combined datasets. The results suggest that a single multi-domain model can achieve comparable performance to specialized single-domain models, thereby reducing operational complexity.  The paper also details practical considerations such as data preparation, handling non-extractive predictions, and evaluating different failure modes in LLM-based structured prediction.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Practical Relevance:** The paper addresses a genuine industrial need, making it immediately relevant to practitioners facing similar challenges in deploying ABSA systems. The use of internal datasets adds a degree of authenticity.
    *   **Multi-domain Modeling:** The core finding that a single multi-domain model performs comparably to specialized models is valuable and can lead to significant cost and maintenance benefits in real-world scenarios.
    *   **Detailed Analysis:** The paper provides a detailed breakdown of failure modes and mitigation strategies, offering practical guidance for building robust ABSA pipelines. The handling of non-extractive predictions is a valuable contribution.
    *   **Reproducibility details**: The paper provides useful details about model selection, training setup and data format.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Novelty:** While the application is practical, the core methodology of fine-tuning LLMs for ABSA is not particularly novel. Many prior works have explored similar approaches. The lack of comparison to prior art ABSA systems is noticeable.
    *   **Internal Datasets:** Using internal datasets, while providing real-world relevance, makes it difficult to compare the results directly with other research that uses standard benchmark datasets. Lack of public benchmarks can be a significant obstacle in assessing the true performance.
    *   **Small Model Size:** While the decision to focus on smaller models aligns with deployment constraints, it limits the potential performance gains and makes it difficult to generalize the findings to larger, more capable LLMs.
    *   **Evaluation metrics:** The paper includes "relaxed evaluation" metric that gives partial credits for small differences in opinion expression extraction. This evaluation setting is not common in ABSA research.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper contributes to the understanding of how LLMs can be effectively used for ABSA in industrial settings.
    *   The multi-domain modeling approach offers a practical solution for managing complex taxonomies, a common problem in real-world applications.
    *   The detailed analysis of failure modes and mitigation strategies provides valuable insights for building robust ABSA pipelines.

    However, the lack of comparative results using standard benchmarks and other state-of-the-art approaches limits the significance of the paper to a purely applied setting. There is nothing particularly breakthrough or transformative that could drastically change the direction of the field.

**Score: 6**

**Justification:**

The paper presents a solid and practical application of LLMs for ABSA in an industrial setting. The multi-domain modeling approach is valuable for reducing operational complexity, and the detailed analysis of failure modes provides practical guidance. However, the novelty of the core methodology is limited, and the use of internal datasets makes it difficult to compare the results with other research. Therefore, despite its practical value, the paper's contribution to the broader field is incremental rather than transformative.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Are LLM-generated plain language summaries truly understandable? A large-scale crowdsourced evaluation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10409v1)
- **Authors**: Yue Guo, Jae Ho Sohn, Gondy Leroy, Trevor Cohen
- **Abstract**: Plain language summaries (PLSs) are essential for facilitating effective communication between clinicians and patients by making complex medical information easier for laypeople to understand and act upon. Large language models (LLMs) have recently shown promise in automating PLS generation, but their effectiveness in supporting health information comprehension remains unclear. Prior evaluations have generally relied on automated scores that do not measure understandability directly, or subjective Likert-scale ratings from convenience samples with limited generalizability. To address these gaps, we conducted a large-scale crowdsourced evaluation of LLM-generated PLSs using Amazon Mechanical Turk with 150 participants. We assessed PLS quality through subjective Likert-scale ratings focusing on simplicity, informativeness, coherence, and faithfulness; and objective multiple-choice comprehension and recall measures of reader understanding. Additionally, we examined the alignment between 10 automated evaluation metrics and human judgments. Our findings indicate that while LLMs can generate PLSs that appear indistinguishable from human-written ones in subjective evaluations, human-written PLSs lead to significantly better comprehension. Furthermore, automated evaluation metrics fail to reflect human judgment, calling into question their suitability for evaluating PLSs. This is the first study to systematically evaluate LLM-generated PLSs based on both reader preferences and comprehension outcomes. Our findings highlight the need for evaluation frameworks that move beyond surface-level quality and for generation methods that explicitly optimize for layperson comprehension.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates whether plain language summaries (PLSs) generated by large language models (LLMs) are truly understandable to laypeople. The authors conduct a large-scale crowdsourced evaluation using Amazon Mechanical Turk, comparing LLM-generated PLSs with human-written ones.  They assess PLS quality using both subjective Likert-scale ratings (simplicity, informativeness, coherence, faithfulness) and objective comprehension measures (multiple-choice questions, recall).  The study also examines the alignment between automated evaluation metrics and human judgments.  The key finding is that while LLM-generated PLSs can achieve comparable subjective ratings to human-written PLSs, they lead to significantly worse comprehension as measured by objective tests.  The paper also highlights the inadequacy of commonly used automated evaluation metrics for PLS assessment, demonstrating their poor correlation with human comprehension.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its comprehensive approach to evaluating LLM-generated PLSs.  While previous work has explored LLM-based summarization and simplification, this study stands out because of its large-scale crowdsourced evaluation incorporating both subjective and objective measures, the comparison of various LLM optimization strategies, and the analysis of automated metric alignment.  The specific finding that subjective fluency does not necessarily translate to objective comprehension is valuable.

*   **Significance:**  The findings are significant for the fields of medical communication, natural language processing, and human-computer interaction. It challenges the reliance on automated metrics and subjective assessments in evaluating PLSs generated by LLMs. It underscores the importance of comprehension-centered evaluation protocols that incorporate objective assessments. The research emphasizes the need for LLMs to be explicitly optimized for layperson comprehension, going beyond surface-level readability. Given the increasing use of LLMs for generating patient-facing health information, the practical implications of this work are substantial, potentially impacting how health information is disseminated and understood by the public.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Rigorous Methodology:** The study employs a well-designed crowdsourced evaluation framework with appropriate controls for data quality (attention checks, completion time filtering).
    *   **Large-Scale Evaluation:** The use of 150 participants and 1346 annotations provides greater statistical power and generalizability than many prior studies in this area.
    *   **Comprehensive Assessment:**  The combination of subjective ratings, multiple-choice questions, and recall tasks provides a multifaceted view of PLS quality and comprehension.
    *   **Analysis of Automated Metrics:** The investigation into the alignment between automated metrics and human judgments is particularly valuable, exposing the limitations of existing evaluation tools.
    *   **Clarity and Organization:** The paper is well-written and clearly presents the research questions, methods, results, and conclusions.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Topical Diversity:** The evaluation is based on a sample of 50 abstracts, which may limit the topical diversity of the evaluation, and therefore the generalization.
    *   **MTurk Participant Representation:** While MTurk provides a more diverse participant pool than university-based samples, it may still not fully represent the general population in terms of literacy and health knowledge.
    *   **Lack of Deeper Analysis of LLM Errors:** The paper demonstrates the discrepancy between subjective and objective comprehension but doesn't deeply investigate *why* the LLMs fail.  A more in-depth error analysis of the LLM-generated PLSs could have provided more actionable insights for improving generation strategies.  What specific features or qualities of the text created by the LLMs are impacting comprehension?
    *   **Limited Scope of Metrics:** While multiple metrics are considered, others might reveal more about discrepancies. Perhaps assessing the semantic similarity of content between source document and PLS might have identified where LLMs hallucinate or introduce errors.

*   **Impact and Influence:** This paper is likely to influence future research on PLS generation and evaluation, particularly in the context of LLMs.  It should motivate researchers to move beyond simple readability scores and to prioritize comprehension-centered evaluation. It can also inform the development of more effective generation strategies that explicitly optimize for layperson understanding. Given the current momentum in LLM-based healthcare tools, it is very pertinent and influential.

**Score: 8**

**Justification:**

The paper makes a significant and novel contribution by demonstrating the limitations of LLM-generated PLSs despite their superficial fluency, emphasizing the critical need for objective comprehension-based evaluation. It sheds light on shortcomings in current PLS evaluation methods, potentially influencing the development of more accurate metrics for PLS creation. However, the paper is limited by a fairly small set of source material, and a more in-depth analysis of types of LLM errors is a missed opportunity. While insightful, the research has room to grow.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Learning to Think: Information-Theoretic Reinforcement Fine-Tuning for LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10425v1)
- **Authors**: Jingyao Wang, Wenwen Qiang, Zeen Song, Changwen Zheng, Hui Xiong
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) excel at complex tasks thanks to advances in reasoning abilities. However, existing methods overlook the trade-off between reasoning effectiveness and computational efficiency, often encouraging unnecessarily long reasoning chains and wasting tokens. To address this, we propose Learning to Think (L2T), an information-theoretic reinforcement fine-tuning framework for LLMs to make the models achieve optimal reasoning with fewer tokens. Specifically, L2T treats each query-response interaction as a hierarchical session of multiple episodes and proposes a universal dense process reward, i.e., quantifies the episode-wise information gain in parameters, requiring no extra annotations or task-specific evaluators. We propose a method to quickly estimate this reward based on PAC-Bayes bounds and the Fisher information matrix. Theoretical analyses show that it significantly reduces computational complexity with high estimation accuracy. By immediately rewarding each episode's contribution and penalizing excessive updates, L2T optimizes the model via reinforcement learning to maximize the use of each episode and achieve effective updates. Empirical results on various reasoning benchmarks and base models demonstrate the advantage of L2T across different tasks, boosting both reasoning effectiveness and efficiency.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Learning to Think (L2T), a reinforcement fine-tuning framework designed to improve the reasoning efficiency of Large Language Models (LLMs). L2T addresses the trade-off between reasoning effectiveness and computational cost by focusing on token usage.  It proposes a dense process reward based on information theory that quantifies the information gain in model parameters at each reasoning step (episode), penalizing excessive updates and promoting effective updates. L2T treats the question-answer interaction as a hierarchical session of multiple episodes, rewarding each episode's contribution. The authors estimate this reward efficiently using PAC-Bayes bounds and the Fisher information matrix. The framework is designed to be task-agnostic and does not rely on external annotations. Experiments on various reasoning benchmarks demonstrate that L2T can improve both the accuracy and token efficiency of LLMs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's core novelty lies in its application of information-theoretic principles to design a dense reward function for LLM fine-tuning, specifically targeting the trade-off between accuracy and computational efficiency (token usage). While reinforcement learning and process rewards have been used before in LLM reasoning, the specific formulation of the reward based on parameter information gain, leveraging PAC-Bayes bounds and the Fisher information matrix, and the hierarchical episodic approach appear novel. It's a well-reasoned approach that goes beyond simply adding a length penalty.
*   **Significance:** The problem of LLMs generating unnecessarily long reasoning chains, consuming excessive tokens, is a recognized issue. Addressing this has significant practical implications for reducing computational costs and improving the viability of LLMs in resource-constrained environments. L2T's ability to improve both accuracy and token efficiency represents a significant advancement. By reducing redundant computation and improving reasoning efficiency, this work contributes to making large language models more practical and sustainable. The task-agnostic nature of the approach and the elimination of the need for specialized evaluators also increases its practical significance.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper is well-written and clearly explains the problem, the proposed solution, and the experimental results.
    *   The theoretical justification for the information-theoretic reward is solid and convincing.
    *   The experimental results demonstrate clear improvements in both accuracy and token efficiency across a range of benchmarks and base models.
    *   The ablation studies provide insights into the importance of different components of the L2T framework.
    *   The scalability analysis justifies the efficiency gains by showing speedups of several orders of magnitude through dimension reduction techniques.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The performance gains, while consistent, are moderate. Further exploration of more challenging and diverse tasks would be beneficial.
    *   While task-agnostic, more comparisons to task-specific baselines is needed, and these should be added.
    *   The reliance on a low-rank approximation and Fisher information matrix estimation introduces approximations which may limit performance on particularly complex scenarios. The long tail of token complexity in these models and related edge cases could see greater improvements from an adaptive test time compute approach that uses per-episode results for a more general reward.

*   **Potential Influence:** L2T has the potential to influence future research in LLM fine-tuning by providing a principled and effective approach for optimizing reasoning efficiency. The information-theoretic reward function and the episodic learning framework could inspire new techniques for addressing other challenges in LLM reasoning. Further work could explore the applicability of L2T to other tasks and modalities.

**Rigorous Rationale:**

The paper presents a solid contribution to the field by addressing a relevant and practical problem (token efficiency in LLM reasoning) with a novel and well-justified approach. The empirical results support the effectiveness of the proposed framework. However, the performance gains are not revolutionary, and the limitations of the approximations should be addressed in future work. Given these considerations, a score between 7 and 8 is appropriate.

**Score: 7**
- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Score-based diffusion nowcasting of GOES imagery
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10432v1)
- **Authors**: Randy J. Chase, Katherine Haynes, Lander Ver Hoef, Imme Ebert-Uphoff
- **Abstract**: Clouds and precipitation are important for understanding weather and climate. Simulating clouds and precipitation with traditional numerical weather prediction is challenging because of the sub-grid parameterizations required. Machine learning has been explored for forecasting clouds and precipitation, but early machine learning methods often created blurry forecasts. In this paper we explore a newer method, named score-based diffusion, to nowcast (zero to three hour forecast) clouds and precipitation. We discuss the background and intuition of score-based diffusion models - thus providing a starting point for the community - while exploring the methodology's use for nowcasting geostationary infrared imagery. We experiment with three main types of diffusion models: a standard score-based diffusion model (Diff); a residual correction diffusion model (CorrDiff); and a latent diffusion model (LDM). Our results show that the diffusion models are able to not only advect existing clouds, but also generate and decay clouds, including convective initiation. These results are surprising because the forecasts are initiated with only the past 20 mins of infrared satellite imagery. A case study qualitatively shows the preservation of high resolution features longer into the forecast than a conventional mean-squared error trained U-Net. The best of the three diffusion models tested was the CorrDiff approach, outperforming all other diffusion models, the traditional U-Net, and a persistence forecast by one to two kelvin on root mean squared error. The diffusion models also enable out-of-the-box ensemble generation, which shows skillful calibration, with the spread of the ensemble correlating well to the error.
- **Summary**: **Summary:** The paper explores the application of score-based diffusion models for nowcasting clouds and precipitation using geostationary infrared imagery from the GOES satellite. The authors discuss the challenges of traditional numerical weather prediction, particularly in simulating clouds and precipitation, and highlight the limitations of earlier machine learning methods that produced blurry forecasts. They introduce three diffusion models: a standard score-based diffusion model, a residual correction diffusion model, and a latent diffusion model. The experiments show that these models can not only advect existing clouds but also generate and decay clouds, including initiating convection, using just 20 minutes of prior imagery. The results demonstrate that the residual correction model (CorrDiff) outperformed the conventional U-Net and traditional persistence forecasts in terms of root mean squared error, while also showcasing effective ensemble generation capabilities. **Critical Evaluation:** **Novelty:**   The novelty of this paper lies in its application of score-based diffusion models—which are relatively new in the context of weather forecasting—to the specific problem of nowcasting clouds and precipitation. Unlike previous methods that struggled with quality and clarity in predictions, this approach demonstrates the ability to generate sharper, more accurate forecasts by leveraging advanced machine learning techniques. The introduction of a residual correction mechanism within the diffusion framework is innovative and may be a significant advancement in terms of model refinement. **Strengths:**   - **Innovative Methodology:** The use of score-based diffusion models represents a shift away from traditional numerical models and provides a fresh perspective on weather prediction. - **Effectiveness:** The empirical results indicate that the proposed models outperform established methods in key metrics (e.g., root mean squared error), suggesting practical applicability. - **Quality of Prediction:** The retention of high-resolution features in forecasts indicates that the model provides detailed and reliable information over short time scales, which is critical for operational weather forecasting. - **Ensemble Generation:** Their ability to produce effective ensembles highlights an important feature for uncertainty quantification in forecasts. **Weaknesses:**   - **Generalizability:** While the results are promising, the extent to which these findings can be generalized to other regions or different climatic conditions remains to be seen. - **Compared Methodology:** The comparison with only one conventional model (the U-Net) may limit the thoroughness of the evaluation. Including more diverse benchmarking models could strengthen the argument. - **Computational Cost:** The paper does not address the computational efficiency of these models compared to traditional forecasting methods, which is critical for operational use. **Overall Impact:**   The paper presents a significant advancement in the field of machine learning applications for meteorology by addressing the well-known challenges of cloud simulation with a novel approach. If validated in broader contexts, this work could lead to improvements in real-time weather forecasting, potentially influencing operational protocols. Based on the considerations above, I would assign a score that reflects both the innovation and the initial practical implications while also acknowledging the limitations noted. **Score: 8**
- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### Are Large Language Models Robust in Understanding Code Against Semantics-Preserving Mutations?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10443v1)
- **Authors**: Pedro Orvalho, Marta Kwiatkowska
- **Abstract**: Understanding the reasoning and robustness of Large Language Models (LLMs) is critical for their reliable use in programming tasks. While recent studies have assessed LLMs' ability to predict program outputs, most focus solely on the accuracy of those predictions, without evaluating the reasoning behind them. Moreover, it has been observed on mathematical reasoning tasks that LLMs can arrive at correct answers through flawed logic, raising concerns about similar issues in code understanding. In this work, we evaluate whether state-of-the-art LLMs with up to 8B parameters can reason about Python programs or are simply guessing. We apply five semantics-preserving code mutations: renaming variables, mirroring comparison expressions, swapping if-else branches, converting for loops to while, and loop unrolling. These mutations maintain program semantics while altering its syntax. We evaluated six LLMs and performed a human expert analysis using LiveCodeBench to assess whether the correct predictions are based on sound reasoning. We also evaluated prediction stability across different code mutations on LiveCodeBench and CruxEval. Our findings show that some LLMs, such as Llama3.2, produce correct predictions based on flawed reasoning in up to 61% of cases. Furthermore, LLMs often change predictions in response to our code mutations, indicating limited robustness in their semantic understanding.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the robustness of Large Language Models (LLMs) in understanding code by assessing their ability to predict program outputs after applying semantics-preserving code mutations.  The authors evaluate several LLMs (including CodeGenie, GraniteCode, Qwen2.5-Coder, Mistral, SemCoder, and Llama3.2) on two benchmarks (LiveCodeBench and CruxEval). They apply five types of code mutations: variable renaming, mirroring comparison expressions, swapping if-else branches, converting for loops to while loops, and unrolling loops. The goal is to determine whether LLMs truly understand code semantics or merely rely on syntactic patterns. The authors conduct an expert analysis of LLMs' reasoning and analyze prediction consistency across the different mutations.  The results indicate that some LLMs produce correct outputs based on flawed reasoning, and many are sensitive to syntactic changes, suggesting a lack of semantic robustness. The paper also explores the benefits of interactive querying to improve prediction accuracy and logical reasoning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of using semantics-preserving mutations to test the *understanding* (not just accuracy) of LLMs in a coding context is reasonably novel and builds upon previous work that has focused on adversarial examples or robustness to syntax changes *not* explicitly designed to preserve semantics. The paper adds to this line of research by looking at specific types of mutations and their impact on LLMs' reasoning *quality*, not simply if the output is right or wrong. Also, the methodology of manual expert evaluation is commendable, given that auto-assessment is inherently unable to capture the quality of the reasoning.

*   **Significance:** The findings are significant because they highlight a crucial limitation of current LLMs used in code-related tasks. If LLMs are merely pattern-matching on code syntax rather than truly understanding semantics, their reliability in critical software development tasks is questionable. This insight has significant implications for how LLMs are used in areas such as debugging, code generation, and automated review, as it cautions against blind trust in their outputs. Demonstrating the influence of interactive querying is also valuable, providing a direction for improving LLM performance and reliability. The results are also useful from a research perspective because they suggest where efforts may be best focused: improving LLM reasoning rather than focusing exclusively on improving overall predictive ability.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The use of semantics-preserving mutations is a clever approach to assess code understanding versus pattern recognition.
    *   The benchmarks are standard and relevant.
    *   The inclusion of both coding-specific LLMs and a general-purpose LLM provides a useful comparison.
    *   The manual expert analysis adds a crucial layer of insight that automated evaluation alone cannot provide.
    *   The exploration of interactive querying offers a practical direction for improvement.
    *   The paper is generally well-written and the experiments are relatively easy to replicate.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The number of LLMs evaluated is relatively small, and only one general-purpose model was included, reducing the generality of conclusions.  More diverse model architectures would benefit the study.
    *   While the types of semantics-preserving mutations are commonly used, the paper does not discuss how these were chosen. A discussion of the completeness of mutations or why these are important for software engineering tasks would be beneficial.
    *   The expert analysis, while valuable, introduces a degree of subjectivity.  The criteria used for determining 'flawed reasoning' could be more clearly defined.
    *   It is not clear from the paper how 'incorrect' code was assessed. Was it by means of unit tests or other means? This should be made more explicit.
    *   While interactive querying demonstrates a way to improve reasoning, the experimental setup of only 5 rounds of back-and-forth is insufficient to truly establish how to best use prompting techniques to improve LLM understanding.
    *   The paper states that MISTRAL and SEMCODER cannot use iterative querying. What is the basis of that claim? There is nothing inherent in those models that prevents interaction.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research in the following ways:
    *   Motivating the development of more robust evaluation methodologies for code-related LLMs.
    *   Encouraging research focused on improving the semantic understanding of LLMs rather than simply increasing their accuracy.
    *   Inspiring the design of new training techniques that explicitly promote robustness to code mutations.
    *   Further exploration of interactive querying techniques to improve LLM reasoning and reliability in software development tasks.

**Justification of Score:**

Considering the novelty, significance, strengths, and weaknesses, the paper warrants a score of **7**. The core idea of using semantics-preserving mutations to assess code understanding is strong and the results are insightful, demonstrating real limitations in existing LLMs. However, the limited scope of the models evaluated, a somewhat subjective element in the analysis, and a degree of incompleteness in the discussion of code mutation and code assessment hold back the paper from achieving a higher score. Nevertheless, the findings are important and are very likely to influence future research in this area.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 7/10

### Reinforcing the Diffusion Chain of Lateral Thought with Diffusion Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10446v1)
- **Authors**: Zemin Huang, Zhiyang Chen, Zijun Wang, Tiancheng Li, Guo-Jun Qi
- **Abstract**: We introduce the \emph{Diffusion Chain of Lateral Thought (DCoLT)}, a reasoning framework for diffusion language models. DCoLT treats each intermediate step in the reverse diffusion process as a latent "thinking" action and optimizes the entire reasoning trajectory to maximize the reward on the correctness of the final answer with outcome-based Reinforcement Learning (RL). Unlike traditional Chain-of-Thought (CoT) methods that follow a causal, linear thinking process, DCoLT allows bidirectional, non-linear reasoning with no strict rule on grammatical correctness amid its intermediate steps of thought. We implement DCoLT on two representative Diffusion Language Models (DLMs). First, we choose SEDD as a representative continuous-time discrete diffusion model, where its concrete score derives a probabilistic policy to maximize the RL reward over the entire sequence of intermediate diffusion steps. We further consider the discrete-time masked diffusion language model -- LLaDA, and find that the order to predict and unmask tokens plays an essential role to optimize its RL action resulting from the ranking-based Unmasking Policy Module (UPM) defined by the Plackett-Luce model. Experiments on both math and code generation tasks show that using only public data and 16 H800 GPUs, DCoLT-reinforced DLMs outperform other DLMs trained by SFT or RL or even both. Notably, DCoLT-reinforced LLaDA boosts its reasoning accuracy by +9.8%, +5.7%, +11.4%, +19.5% on GSM8K, MATH, MBPP, and HumanEval.
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Reinforcing the Diffusion Chain of Lateral Thought with Diffusion Language Models".

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Diffusion Chain of Lateral Thought (DCoLT), a novel reasoning framework for diffusion language models (DLMs). DCoLT treats each intermediate step in the reverse diffusion process as a latent "thinking" action and optimizes the entire reasoning trajectory to maximize the reward on the correctness of the final answer using reinforcement learning (RL). Unlike traditional Chain-of-Thought (CoT) methods, DCoLT allows bidirectional, non-linear reasoning without strict grammatical correctness constraints during intermediate steps. The authors implement DCoLT on two representative DLMs: SEDD (a continuous-time discrete diffusion model) and LLaDA (a discrete-time masked diffusion language model). They introduce a probabilistic policy for SEDD and a Plackett-Luce model-based Unmasking Policy Module (UPM) for LLaDA to optimize the RL action. Experiments on math and code generation tasks demonstrate that DCoLT-reinforced DLMs outperform other DLMs trained with SFT or RL, even those trained on significantly more data.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a genuinely novel approach to reasoning in DLMs. The concept of "lateral thought" in diffusion models and using RL to reinforce the entire diffusion chain, rather than individual steps, is a significant departure from existing CoT methods. The specific implementations, like the UPM for LLaDA, also introduce unique technical contributions.

*   **Significance:** The experimental results demonstrate a substantial performance boost, particularly for LLaDA. The fact that DCoLT-reinforced DLMs can achieve state-of-the-art results using only public data and fewer computational resources than some autoregressive models trained on proprietary data highlights the practical significance of the approach. The performance improvements across GSM8K, MATH, MBPP, and HumanEval are compelling.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The core concept of lateral thought in diffusion models is innovative.
    *   The use of outcome-based RL effectively guides the model to discover diverse reasoning trajectories.
    *   The implementations on SEDD and LLaDA are well-designed and showcase the framework's adaptability.
    *   The experimental results are strong and consistently demonstrate the benefits of DCoLT.
    *   The analysis of the thinking process in SEDD and LLaDA+DCoLT provides valuable insights into the model's behavior.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the experimental results are impressive, the computational cost of DCoLT training, especially with multi-step generations, is a potential limitation. Future work could investigate techniques to improve the efficiency of RL optimization.
    *   The discussion of broader impacts and safeguards is relatively brief. A more in-depth analysis of potential misuse and mitigation strategies would strengthen the paper.
    *   While the paper showcases the models' efficacy on multiple benchmarks, some of those are becoming overly saturated, and more challenging, more diverse benchmarks, including those incorporating world knowledge or multi-modality, could be insightful.

*   **Potential Influence:** DCoLT has the potential to significantly influence the field of reasoning in language models, especially within the domain of diffusion models. It provides a new framework for thinking about reasoning as a holistic process and opens up new avenues for research in areas such as:
    *   Developing more efficient RL algorithms for training DCoLT.
    *   Exploring different mechanisms for promoting diversity in reasoning trajectories.
    *   Applying DCoLT to other types of DLMs and reasoning tasks.
    *   Investigating the transferability of DCoLT-trained models to different domains.
    *   Combining DCoLT with external knowledge sources.

*   **Rigorous Rationale:**
    The paper's impact is significant, but not revolutionary enough to merit an exceptionally high score (9 or 10). The reliance on outcome-based RL might still present challenges for tasks where verifiable correctness is difficult to define. Furthermore, while the improvements are substantial, the computational expense of the approach needs to be addressed before it can be widely adopted. Therefore, a score of 8 is a more fitting representation of the paper's contribution.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Superposition Yields Robust Neural Scaling
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10465v1)
- **Authors**: Yizhou liu, Ziming Liu, Jeff Gore
- **Abstract**: The success of today's large language models (LLMs) depends on the observation that larger models perform better. However, the origin of this neural scaling law -- the finding that loss decreases as a power law with model size -- remains unclear. Starting from two empirical principles -- that LLMs represent more things than the model dimensions (widths) they have (i.e., representations are superposed), and that words or concepts in language occur with varying frequencies -- we constructed a toy model to study the loss scaling with model size. We found that when superposition is weak, meaning only the most frequent features are represented without interference, the scaling of loss with model size depends on the underlying feature frequency; if feature frequencies follow a power law, so does the loss. In contrast, under strong superposition, where all features are represented but overlap with each other, the loss becomes inversely proportional to the model dimension across a wide range of feature frequency distributions. This robust scaling behavior is explained geometrically: when many more vectors are packed into a lower dimensional space, the interference (squared overlaps) between vectors scales inversely with that dimension. We then analyzed four families of open-sourced LLMs and found that they exhibit strong superposition and quantitatively match the predictions of our toy model. The Chinchilla scaling law turned out to also agree with our results. We conclude that representation superposition is an important mechanism underlying the observed neural scaling laws. We anticipate that these insights will inspire new training strategies and model architectures to achieve better performance with less computation and fewer parameters.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Superposition Yields Robust Neural Scaling":

**Summary:**

This paper investigates the underlying mechanisms of neural scaling laws, specifically focusing on the role of *representation superposition* – the idea that LLMs represent more features than the model's dimensionality would seemingly allow. The authors construct a toy model incorporating feature frequencies and the degree of superposition.  They find that in a "weak superposition" regime, the loss scaling with model size is sensitive to the feature frequency distribution. In contrast, under "strong superposition," loss scales inversely with model dimension regardless of the frequency distribution, providing a more robust scaling behavior.  They then analyze open-sourced LLMs, finding evidence of strong superposition and quantitative agreement with their toy model's predictions. The Chinchilla scaling law is also shown to align with the strong superposition results. The authors conclude that superposition is crucial for neural scaling laws and suggest that promoting superposition could improve model efficiency.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper makes a valuable contribution by explicitly investigating superposition in the context of neural scaling laws. While the concept of superposition itself isn't entirely new (as the authors acknowledge, citing Anthropic's work), this paper connects it directly and quantitatively to the observed scaling phenomena in LLMs. This is an important step because previous explanations of scaling laws haven't thoroughly accounted for how representation limitations impact final loss.

**Significance:** The paper's significance stems from its potential to inform more efficient model design and training strategies. By demonstrating the importance of superposition, it suggests that architectures and training techniques that encourage efficient representation learning may be key to further improvements in LLMs. If future work can build upon these insights to create models that achieve better performance with fewer parameters, it would be a significant advance.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear and focused research question:** The paper clearly identifies the gap in understanding the role of superposition in scaling laws and provides a targeted approach to address it.
*   **Rigorous methodology:** The toy model is well-defined and allows for controlled experimentation.  The analysis of open-source LLMs provides empirical support for the model's predictions.
*   **Quantitative analysis:** The paper goes beyond qualitative arguments, providing quantitative comparisons between the toy model, theoretical predictions, and real LLM behavior.
*   **Strong connection between theory and experiments:** The results are used to predict the exponent in LLM scaling laws.
*   **Well-written and clearly presented:** the paper is clear and well-organized.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Toy Model Simplifications:** The toy model, while useful, necessarily simplifies the complexities of real LLMs. The authors acknowledge these limitations, including the lack of transformer layers and the use of a squared error loss function instead of cross-entropy. There is a significant gap between the toy model and the full complexity of an LLM, which does limit the real-world applicability of the results.
*   **Lack of Causation:** The work focuses on *correlating* superposition with good scaling, not *proving* that enhancing superposition *causes* better scaling.  While suggestive, more direct experimental evidence is needed to support the causal claim.
*   **Limited scope of LLM Analysis:**  The analysis of LLMs is somewhat limited to examining weight matrix statistics. Deeper investigation into how superposition manifests within the transformer layers themselves would be valuable.
*   **Future Directions:** Although the conclusions are clearly stated, many of the implications are only partially developed. In particular, it is not shown experimentally that promoting superposition leads to any benefit.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a valuable link between representation superposition and neural scaling laws, providing both a theoretical framework and empirical evidence. However, the simplified nature of the toy model and the correlational (rather than causal) nature of the LLM analysis limit the immediate impact of the work. While the paper could inform future research directions, the benefits are not immediately apparent and require additional research.

Score: 6.5

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### AI Agents vs. Agentic AI: A Conceptual Taxonomy, Applications and Challenge
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10468v1)
- **Authors**: Ranjan Sapkota, Konstantinos I. Roumeliotis, Manoj Karkee
- **Abstract**: This study critically distinguishes between AI Agents and Agentic AI, offering a structured conceptual taxonomy, application mapping, and challenge analysis to clarify their divergent design philosophies and capabilities. We begin by outlining the search strategy and foundational definitions, characterizing AI Agents as modular systems driven by Large Language Models (LLMs) and Large Image Models (LIMs) for narrow, task-specific automation. Generative AI is positioned as a precursor, with AI Agents advancing through tool integration, prompt engineering, and reasoning enhancements. In contrast, Agentic AI systems represent a paradigmatic shift marked by multi-agent collaboration, dynamic task decomposition, persistent memory, and orchestrated autonomy. Through a sequential evaluation of architectural evolution, operational mechanisms, interaction styles, and autonomy levels, we present a comparative analysis across both paradigms. Application domains such as customer support, scheduling, and data summarization are contrasted with Agentic AI deployments in research automation, robotic coordination, and medical decision support. We further examine unique challenges in each paradigm including hallucination, brittleness, emergent behavior, and coordination failure and propose targeted solutions such as ReAct loops, RAG, orchestration layers, and causal modeling. This work aims to provide a definitive roadmap for developing robust, scalable, and explainable AI agent and Agentic AI-driven systems. >AI Agents, Agent-driven, Vision-Language-Models, Agentic AI Decision Support System, Agentic-AI Applications
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper provides a structured analysis contrasting AI Agents and Agentic AI, clarifying their distinct design philosophies, capabilities, applications, and challenges. It starts by defining AI Agents as modular systems primarily driven by Large Language Models (LLMs) or Large Image Models (LIMs) for specific task automation, in contrast to Generative AI as a precursor. Agentic AI, on the other hand, represents a shift towards multi-agent collaboration, dynamic task decomposition, persistent memory, and autonomous operation. The paper then compares the two paradigms across various dimensions including architectural evolution, operational mechanisms, interaction styles, and autonomy levels. It maps applications ranging from customer support (AI Agents) to research automation or robotic coordination (Agentic AI). It further explores challenges including hallucination, brittleness, emergent behaviors, and proposes solutions like ReAct loops, Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG), orchestration layers, and causal modeling.  The paper aims to offer a roadmap for developing robust, scalable, and explainable AI systems.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary strength lies in its clear and comprehensive taxonomy differentiating AI Agents and Agentic AI. While individual aspects of these systems are known, the explicit comparison, architectural decomposition, and application mapping are valuable. The analysis of the challenges and the proposed solutions consolidates existing knowledge and positions them in the context of these two paradigms, leading to a somewhat novel synthesis. The paper also points out that research activity in these fields has seen a significant rise since the beginning of the ChatGPT era, and there has been a demand in clarifying the conceptual difference between these two concepts.
*   **Significance:**  The paper is significant because it provides a much-needed structured framework for understanding the rapidly evolving landscape of AI-driven systems. It clarifies terminology which is often loosely and inconsistently used in the field. This framework can help researchers and practitioners better align their design choices with problem complexity, and benchmark different approaches in a more informed manner. The identification of key limitations and potential solutions can guide future research directions.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Taxonomy:** The paper excels in providing a clear taxonomy and comparison along several key dimensions, which is helpful for navigating a complex area.
    *   **Comprehensive Coverage:** The paper covers a broad range of topics, from foundational concepts to advanced applications and emerging challenges.
    *   **Practical Orientation:** By outlining practical solutions and highlighting successful applications, the paper offers actionable insights for developing AI-driven systems.
    *   **Balanced Perspective:** Acknowledging the limitations of both AI Agents and Agentic AI creates a more objective perspective and avoids overly optimistic claims.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Empirical Validation:** The paper primarily relies on literature synthesis rather than original empirical studies or experiments. Although the framework can be considered a form of synthesis, it lacks supporting data.
    *   **Generality and Lack of Depth:**  Due to the broad scope, the analysis of specific challenges and solutions could be more detailed. For instance, the section on causal modeling could delve more deeply into specific causal inference techniques suitable for these systems.
    *   **Potential for Oversimplification:** By creating clear categories, the paper risks oversimplifying what is a complex, evolving landscape. There's potential for hybrid systems and blurring of the lines between the defined categories. For instance, the 'Generative Agent' can be seen as something hybrid.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper is likely to have significant influence, especially as a reference point for future research and development. The structured taxonomy and challenge analysis will assist in defining research questions and guide design decisions. It will particularly benefit those new to the field who need a conceptual foundation.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:**

The paper merits a score of 7 for its strong synthesis of existing knowledge and its clear, structured taxonomy of AI Agents and Agentic AI. It provides a valuable service to the community by disambiguating terms and mapping the landscape. However, the lack of original empirical data, some degree of generality, and the potential for oversimplification prevent it from achieving a higher score. The paper's greatest strength is that it consolidates existing ideas and puts them in context, rather than presenting groundbreaking, entirely novel findings. That said, the synthesis itself adds value and justifies a solid score.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Large Language Models for Cancer Communication: Evaluating Linguistic Quality, Safety, and Accessibility in Generative AI
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10472v1)
- **Authors**: Agnik Saha, Victoria Churchill, Anny D. Rodriguez, Ugur Kursuncu, Muhammed Y. Idris
- **Abstract**: Effective communication about breast and cervical cancers remains a persistent health challenge, with significant gaps in public understanding of cancer prevention, screening, and treatment, potentially leading to delayed diagnoses and inadequate treatments. This study evaluates the capabilities and limitations of Large Language Models (LLMs) in generating accurate, safe, and accessible cancer-related information to support patient understanding. We evaluated five general-purpose and three medical LLMs using a mixed-methods evaluation framework across linguistic quality, safety and trustworthiness, and communication accessibility and affectiveness. Our approach utilized quantitative metrics, qualitative expert ratings, and statistical analysis using Welch's ANOVA, Games-Howell, and Hedges' g. Our results show that general-purpose LLMs produced outputs of higher linguistic quality and affectiveness, while medical LLMs demonstrate greater communication accessibility. However, medical LLMs tend to exhibit higher levels of potential harm, toxicity, and bias, reducing their performance in safety and trustworthiness. Our findings indicate a duality between domain-specific knowledge and safety in health communications. The results highlight the need for intentional model design with targeted improvements, particularly in mitigating harm and bias, and improving safety and affectiveness. This study provides a comprehensive evaluation of LLMs for cancer communication, offering critical insights for improving AI-generated health content and informing future development of accurate, safe, and accessible digital health tools.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Large Language Models for Cancer Communication: Evaluating Linguistic Quality, Safety, and Accessibility in Generative AI" investigates the effectiveness of both general-purpose and specialized medical Large Language Models (LLMs) in generating accurate, safe, and accessible information about breast and cervical cancers. It uses a mixed-methods approach, including quantitative metrics (ROUGE, BERTScore, toxicity scores, readability indices) and qualitative expert ratings, to evaluate the LLMs across three dimensions: linguistic quality, safety & trustworthiness, and communication accessibility & affectiveness.  The study finds that general-purpose LLMs, particularly Llama 3 and Gemma, excel in linguistic quality and affective communication. Medical LLMs show better communication accessibility (lower reading levels). However, medical LLMs also exhibit higher toxicity and bias.  The research highlights a trade-off between domain-specific knowledge and safety, pointing to the need for improved model design to mitigate harm, bias, and improve safety and affectiveness in AI-generated health content.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper offers a valuable contribution by systematically evaluating both general-purpose and specialized medical LLMs specifically for breast and cervical cancer communication. While there are studies evaluating LLMs in healthcare, fewer focus specifically on the cancer communication context and conduct a head-to-head comparison of diverse models with specific focus on linguistic quality, safety and trustworthiness, and communication accessibility and affectiveness. The use of in-context impersonation to evaluate bias is a useful method.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial need in healthcare: ensuring that AI-driven tools do not exacerbate existing health disparities or contribute to patient harm. By identifying the strengths and limitations of various LLMs, the study provides critical insights for improving AI-generated health content and informing the future development of digital health tools. The findings could influence how these models are deployed in clinical settings and used to educate patients.

**Strengths:**

*   **Comprehensive Evaluation Framework:** The study uses a well-defined and multi-faceted evaluation framework that incorporates both quantitative and qualitative measures. This approach allows for a more nuanced and thorough assessment of the LLMs' performance.
*   **Head-to-Head Comparison:** The paper directly compares the performance of general-purpose and specialized medical LLMs, providing insights into their relative strengths and weaknesses.
*   **Focus on Safety and Trustworthiness:** The study places significant emphasis on evaluating the safety and trustworthiness of LLM-generated content, which is a crucial consideration in healthcare applications.  The use of bias detection methods adds to the significance of the paper.
*   **Clear and Actionable Findings:** The paper's findings are clearly articulated and provide actionable insights for improving the design and deployment of LLMs for cancer communication.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope of Datasets and Models:** The study focuses on a specific set of open-source models and benchmark datasets. The results may not generalize to proprietary models or real-world patient interactions. It is also worthwhile evaluating the models in additional languages other than English.
*   **Potential Dataset Biases:** The paper acknowledges the possibility of dataset biases, which could influence the results. These biases may arise from the topic or language distribution in the datasets.  The paper could benefit from further analysis to quantify the effects of the dataset biases.
*   **Qualitative Measures Subjectivity:** Expert annotations are always subject to inherent subjectivity and differences in interpretation, even when using a structured rubric. The paper acknowledged the subjectivity in the qualitative assessments.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper's findings can inform the development and deployment of LLMs for cancer communication by:

*   Highlighting the need for intentional model design with targeted improvements in mitigating harm and bias.
*   Encouraging the development of hybrid approaches that combine neural and symbolic reasoning.
*   Emphasizing the importance of rigorous evaluation before deploying LLMs in clinical settings.

**Justification:**

The paper represents a valuable contribution to the field of AI in healthcare. It provides a systematic and comprehensive evaluation of LLMs for cancer communication, addressing a critical need for safe, accurate, and accessible health information. While there are some limitations, the paper's findings offer actionable insights for improving the design and deployment of these models.

**Score: 7** The paper makes a solid and important contribution to the field. While not groundbreaking, the methodical evaluation framework, actionable findings, and critical discussion warrant a high score.
- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Fine-tuning Diffusion Policies with Backpropagation Through Diffusion Timesteps
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10482v1)
- **Authors**: Ningyuan Yang, Jiaxuan Gao, Feng Gao, Yi Wu, Chao Yu
- **Abstract**: Diffusion policies, widely adopted in decision-making scenarios such as robotics, gaming and autonomous driving, are capable of learning diverse skills from demonstration data due to their high representation power. However, the sub-optimal and limited coverage of demonstration data could lead to diffusion policies that generate sub-optimal trajectories and even catastrophic failures. While reinforcement learning (RL)-based fine-tuning has emerged as a promising solution to address these limitations, existing approaches struggle to effectively adapt Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) to diffusion models. This challenge stems from the computational intractability of action likelihood estimation during the denoising process, which leads to complicated optimization objectives. In our experiments starting from randomly initialized policies, we find that online tuning of Diffusion Policies demonstrates much lower sample efficiency compared to directly applying PPO on MLP policies (MLP+PPO). To address these challenges, we introduce NCDPO, a novel framework that reformulates Diffusion Policy as a noise-conditioned deterministic policy. By treating each denoising step as a differentiable transformation conditioned on pre-sampled noise, NCDPO enables tractable likelihood evaluation and gradient backpropagation through all diffusion timesteps. Our experiments demonstrate that NCDPO achieves sample efficiency comparable to MLP+PPO when training from scratch, outperforming existing methods in both sample efficiency and final performance across diverse benchmarks, including continuous robot control and multi-agent game scenarios. Furthermore, our experimental results show that our method is robust to the number denoising timesteps in the Diffusion Policy.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Noise-Conditioned Diffusion Policy Optimization (NCDPO), a novel reinforcement learning (RL) algorithm designed for fine-tuning diffusion policies. Diffusion policies, known for their ability to learn diverse skills from demonstration data, can suffer from sub-optimal performance due to limited or poor-quality demonstrations. Existing RL fine-tuning methods, like DPPO, face challenges in efficiently adapting PPO to diffusion models because of the computational complexity of estimating action likelihoods during the denoising process.

NCDPO addresses this by reformulating the diffusion policy as a noise-conditioned deterministic policy. It treats each denoising step as a differentiable transformation conditioned on pre-sampled noise, enabling tractable likelihood evaluation and gradient backpropagation through all diffusion timesteps. The paper shows that NCDPO achieves sample efficiency comparable to training MLP policies with PPO from scratch and outperforms existing methods in terms of sample efficiency and final performance across various benchmarks, including robot control and multi-agent games. The method also demonstrates robustness to the number of denoising timesteps.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of reframing the diffusion policy denoising process as a noise-conditioned deterministic process is a significant contribution. It elegantly addresses the intractability of likelihood evaluation inherent in the DPPO approach. Backpropagation Through Diffusion Timesteps (BPDT) is a clever way to improve performance, which is also novel. The overall combination of techniques leading to NCDPO represents a notable advance.

*   **Significance:**  The improved sample efficiency and performance demonstrated by NCDPO have potentially significant implications. Overcoming the sample efficiency challenges in RL fine-tuning of diffusion policies opens up opportunities for more practical and effective application of these powerful policy classes in robotics, gaming, autonomous driving, and other decision-making tasks. The experimental results strongly support the claim that NCDPO is a superior fine-tuning method.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:**  The paper clearly identifies and articulates the challenges of fine-tuning diffusion policies with RL, particularly the sample efficiency bottleneck.
    *   **Elegant Solution:** NCDPO offers a theoretically sound and practically effective approach to addressing these challenges.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The extensive experimental evaluation, covering a diverse set of environments and comparisons against multiple baselines, provides compelling evidence for the effectiveness of NCDPO.
    *   **Robustness Analysis:** The ablation studies demonstrate the robustness of the method to the number of denoising steps.
    *   **Good writing:** The writing is clear and concise.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Real-World Validation:**  The paper primarily focuses on simulated environments. While the results are promising, the true potential of NCDPO will need to be validated through sim-to-real transfer and real-world robotic experiments. The method does not consider off-policy RL approaches to further increase sample efficiency, such as using the provided initial policy for exploration. It would also be interesting to see the effect of using the reward signal when pretraining the policy, rather than simple behavior cloning.

*   **Potential Influence:** NCDPO has the potential to significantly influence the field by providing a more practical and efficient approach to leveraging diffusion policies in RL settings. The noise-conditioned deterministic policy reformulation may inspire further research into alternative representations and optimization techniques for diffusion models in decision-making.

**Justification for Score:**

Based on the critical evaluation above, the paper demonstrates substantial novelty and significance within the field of reinforcement learning and diffusion models. The elegant solution, strong empirical results, and potential for real-world impact warrant a high score. Despite the lack of real-world experiments and some off-policy RL considerations, the methodological contribution and performance improvements are significant enough to merit a top score, albeit not the highest.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### Campus AI vs Commercial AI: A Late-Breaking Study on How LLM As-A-Service Customizations Shape Trust and Usage Patterns
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10490v1)
- **Authors**: Leon Hannig, Annika Bush, Meltem Aksoy, Steffen Becker, Greta Ontrup
- **Abstract**: As the use of Large Language Models (LLMs) by students, lecturers and researchers becomes more prevalent, universities - like other organizations - are pressed to develop coherent AI strategies. LLMs as-a-Service (LLMaaS) offer accessible pre-trained models, customizable to specific (business) needs. While most studies prioritize data, model, or infrastructure adaptations (e.g., model fine-tuning), we focus on user-salient customizations, like interface changes and corporate branding, which we argue influence users' trust and usage patterns. This study serves as a functional prequel to a large-scale field study in which we examine how students and employees at a German university perceive and use their institution's customized LLMaaS compared to ChatGPT. The goals of this prequel are to stimulate discussions on psychological effects of LLMaaS customizations and refine our research approach through feedback. Our forthcoming findings will deepen the understanding of trust dynamics in LLMs, providing practical guidance for organizations considering LLMaaS deployment.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a late-breaking study proposal examining the impact of customizations in Large Language Models as-a-Service (LLMaaS) on end-user trust, perception, and usage patterns in a university setting.  The authors argue that even user-salient customizations like interface design and corporate branding, which may not directly affect model performance or security, can significantly influence user behavior.  They outline a planned large-scale field study comparing a university's customized LLMaaS to commercial LLMs (like ChatGPT) and pose several hypotheses related to trust, hallucination perception, privacy concerns, and sustainable AI use. The study design involves a survey of students, research staff, and administrative staff. The authors aim to provide evidence-based insights for organizations considering LLMaaS deployment and to contribute to the understanding of trust dynamics in organizational AI systems.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The idea of examining the *psychological* impacts of LLMaaS customizations (especially those that don't directly affect model behavior) is a good one and has some novelty. Most research focuses on fine-tuning and technical aspects.  The paper successfully identifies a gap in the literature. However, the hypotheses themselves are somewhat predictable. While the study explicitly states that the research is primarily concerned with interface and branding customizations, there remains a degree of overlap, which impacts its novelty. The paper makes connections to existing literature on AI trust and organizational trust, which is solid.
* **Significance:**  The study has potential significance for organizations, especially universities, considering deploying LLMaaS. Understanding how users perceive and trust customized systems compared to commercial alternatives is crucial for adoption, effective usage, and mitigating risks associated with overreliance or mistrust. If the results confirm the influence of interface-level cues on trust, it would have practical implications for how these systems are designed and deployed. The potential for influencing sustainable usage patterns (by making token limits visible) is another potentially impactful area. However, much of the significance hinges on how well the theoretical concepts are operationalized within the survey.
* **Strengths:**
    * Clear research question and well-defined scope.
    * The focus on user-salient, non-technical customizations is a valuable contribution.
    * The hypotheses are reasonably grounded in the existing literature.
    * The study design (large-scale field study in a real-world university setting) is appropriate.
    *  Acknowledges limitations, since it is only one institution.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * The paper primarily *proposes* a study and presents a motivation.  There are no results to evaluate.
    * The novelty is good, but incremental.  Many of the hypotheses are intuitive or follow from existing literature on trust and branding.  The added contribution is applying these concepts specifically to LLMaaS and testing them in a university context.
    *  The generalizability of the findings might be limited because it only examines the university members.
    * There is limited discussion of potential confounds or how the study will address them.
    * Limited discussion on potential analysis methods to be used.
    * The description of the university's customized LLMaaS is somewhat vague. More details about the specific customizations (e.g., exact wording of warnings) would strengthen the proposal.
    * The paper claims to provide design recommendations for organizational AI systems, but it doesn't deliver the recommendations, since this is merely a proposal.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of **6**.

* **Reasons for this score:** The paper has a good idea with some novelty and potential significance. It addresses a relevant issue, explores a gap in the literature, and offers a practical real-world study design. However, the paper is primarily a *proposal*. The impact depends heavily on the results of the study, and the hypotheses, while grounded, aren't particularly groundbreaking on their own. The weaknesses related to potential confounds and analysis methods further temper the assessment. Because of this, it does not merit a higher rating. A slightly higher rating could be possible if the university's customizations were well-developed.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CY
- **Score**: 6/10

### CL-RAG: Bridging the Gap in Retrieval-Augmented Generation with Curriculum Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10493v1)
- **Authors**: Shaohan Wang, Licheng Zhang, Zheren Fu, Zhendong Mao
- **Abstract**: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) is an effective method to enhance the capabilities of large language models (LLMs). Existing methods focus on optimizing the retriever or generator in the RAG system by directly utilizing the top-k retrieved documents.
