However, the documents effectiveness are various significantly across user queries, i.e. some documents provide valuable knowledge while others totally lack critical information. It hinders the retriever and generator's adaptation during training. Inspired by human cognitive learning, curriculum learning trains models using samples progressing from easy to difficult, thus enhancing their generalization ability, and we integrate this effective paradigm to the training of the RAG system. In this paper, we propose a multi-stage Curriculum Learning based RAG system training framework, named CL-RAG. We first construct training data with multiple difficulty levels for the retriever and generator separately through sample evolution. Then, we train the model in stages based on the curriculum learning approach, thereby optimizing the overall performance and generalization of the RAG system more effectively. Our CL-RAG framework demonstrates consistent effectiveness across four open-domain QA datasets, achieving performance gains of 2% to 4% over multiple advanced methods.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces CL-RAG, a curriculum learning-based framework for training Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems. It addresses the issue of inconsistent document quality retrieved by RAG systems, which can hinder the training of both the retriever and the generator. CL-RAG constructs training data with varying difficulty levels by rewriting query-enhanced golden documents and counterfactual distractor documents. It then trains the model in stages, progressing from easy to difficult samples, inspired by human cognitive learning.  For the retriever, it employs the well-trained RALM to assess and re-rank documents, constructing training data with an easy-to-difficult progression.  The method is evaluated on four open-domain question-answering datasets, demonstrating improved performance compared to existing RAG methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in integrating curriculum learning into the RAG training process. While curriculum learning has been explored in other NLP tasks, its application to RAG, particularly with the proposed data construction and staged training approaches for both retriever and generator, represents a tangible contribution. The use of the RALM itself to guide retriever training is a logical and potentially powerful extension.
* **Significance:** RAG is a prevalent technique for enhancing LLMs with external knowledge. Improving the training process of RAG systems is significant because it directly translates to better performance in knowledge-intensive tasks.  The performance gains reported, ranging from 2% to 4% over baselines on established QA datasets, are noteworthy and suggest practical value. The study of noise robustness is also relevant and adds to the value of the findings.
* **Strengths:**
    * The paper is well-written and clearly articulates the problem, proposed solution, and experimental setup.
    * The explanation of how the difficulty levels are constructed for both the retriever and generator is thorough and makes intuitive sense.
    * The empirical results demonstrate consistent improvements across multiple datasets, bolstering the credibility of the approach.
    * The ablation studies provide insights into the importance of each training stage, helping to understand the framework's inner workings.
    * The noise robustness tests are useful for understanding the reliability of the approach in real-world settings.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * While the performance gains are significant, they are not transformative.  The study doesn't fundamentally change the landscape of RAG.  The gains fall in the range of previously established methods which means it is an incremental improvement over existing techniques
    * The specific data augmentation and rewriting techniques employed might be somewhat ad-hoc.  A more systematic exploration of different techniques and their impact would strengthen the paper.
    * The experimental setup utilizes LLaMA3-8B, which while modern, is not state-of-the-art and is limited in comparison to the scale of models at the frontier. There is no guarantee that the benefits of curriculum learning will scale to the larger models.
    * The study could benefit from a deeper analysis of *why* the curriculum learning approach is effective.  Are the retriever and generator learning fundamentally different things when trained with this method compared to existing methods?  The paper provides performance numbers, but a more qualitative analysis would be beneficial.
    * The limitations section is rather brief. It mainly discusses the LLM preference measurement and limited iteration. It needs to be a more detailed section about where the proposed framework is expected to fail or where it is not suited.

**Justification for the Score:**

The paper makes a useful and practical contribution to the RAG research area by introducing and evaluating a curriculum learning approach.  The empirical results support the effectiveness of the proposed CL-RAG framework. While the novelty is incremental and the limitations need further exploration, the paper offers a well-executed study with clear results. The gains observed are significant, but not transformative.

### Can You Really Trust Code Copilots? Evaluating Large Language Models from a Code Security Perspective
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10494v1)
- **Authors**: Yutao Mou, Xiao Deng, Yuxiao Luo, Shikun Zhang, Wei Ye
- **Abstract**: Code security and usability are both essential for various coding assistant applications driven by large language models (LLMs). Current code security benchmarks focus solely on single evaluation task and paradigm, such as code completion and generation, lacking comprehensive assessment across dimensions like secure code generation, vulnerability repair and discrimination. In this paper, we first propose CoV-Eval, a multi-task benchmark covering various tasks such as code completion, vulnerability repair, vulnerability detection and classification, for comprehensive evaluation of LLM code security. Besides, we developed VC-Judge, an improved judgment model that aligns closely with human experts and can review LLM-generated programs for vulnerabilities in a more efficient and reliable way. We conduct a comprehensive evaluation of 20 proprietary and open-source LLMs. Overall, while most LLMs identify vulnerable codes well, they still tend to generate insecure codes and struggle with recognizing specific vulnerability types and performing repairs. Extensive experiments and qualitative analyses reveal key challenges and optimization directions, offering insights for future research in LLM code security.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary**

The paper introduces CoV-Eval, a multi-task benchmark designed to comprehensively evaluate the code security of Large Language Models (LLMs).  It covers code completion, vulnerability repair, vulnerability detection, and vulnerability classification tasks, spanning 18 vulnerability types in different programming languages.  The authors also propose VC-Judge, an improved LLM-based judgment model designed to more reliably and efficiently assess the security of LLM-generated code.  The paper evaluates 20 LLMs (both proprietary and open-source) using CoV-Eval, revealing that while many LLMs can identify vulnerable code, they struggle to generate secure code or effectively repair vulnerabilities. The authors provide insights into the challenges faced by LLMs in code security and suggest potential optimization directions.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Benchmark:** CoV-Eval fills a gap in existing code security evaluation, which often focuses on single tasks. Its multi-task nature provides a more realistic and thorough assessment of LLM capabilities.
    *   **Improved Evaluation:** VC-Judge represents an advancement over traditional static analysis and previous LLM-based evaluation, aligning more closely with human expertise, which should improve the reliability of the assessment.
    *   **Extensive Empirical Analysis:** The evaluation of 20 LLMs provides a broad understanding of the current state of LLM code security.
    *   **Practical Insights:** The paper identifies key challenges and offers optimization directions that can guide future research and development efforts in this area.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on LLM for Data Synthesis:** Vul-Evol, while intended to create more complex scenarios, depends on another LLM (GPT-40). While this may be acceptable, this inherently introduces a potential for bias from the LLM. Also, the effectiveness may be limited by the capabilities of the LLM used for data synthesis, and this is acknowledged by the authors.
    *   **Limited Evaluation Scope:** While covering several aspects of code security, the benchmark might not capture all real-world security challenges. Moreover, a more thorough analysis of potential false positives and false negatives by VC-Judge compared to other methods would strengthen the paper.
    *   **Scalability Limitations:** The reliance on LLM-based data synthesis and evaluation components could introduce scalability challenges for larger and more diverse datasets. The effectiveness of LLM-based vulnerability detection remains an open area of research, and VC-Judge, while an improvement, isn't a perfect solution.
    *   **Dataset Limitations**: Although the authors designed the CoV-Eval dataset with 18 vulnerability types, the reliance on GitHub-CWE as the seed set could introduce biases. The dataset and LLM-based vulnerability detection may not cover all possible real-world vulnerabilities.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   The key novelty lies in creating a more comprehensive benchmark than previous works by addressing multiple code security tasks with a single benchmark. This is a much needed step in the evaluation of code generation tools. The improvement in the judge model is a nice improvement, but the dataset is the core contribution here.
    *   The paper addresses a highly relevant topic – the security of LLM-generated code – with significant practical implications. If developers rely on LLMs to generate secure code, this could introduce new vulnerabilities.
    *   The insights gleaned from the evaluation have the potential to influence the design of safer LLMs and coding assistant tools.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   CoV-Eval could become a standard benchmark for evaluating code security in LLMs.
    *   VC-Judge could inspire the development of more reliable LLM-based evaluation methods.
    *   The findings could encourage research into techniques for improving the security of LLM-generated code, such as adversarial training, fine-tuning with secure code data, and better prompt engineering.

*   **Justification for Score:**

The paper provides a valuable contribution to the field by addressing a critical issue in the age of increasingly capable LLMs and generating a novel dataset for this purpose. However, some methodological limitations, especially the dependency on LLMs for data synthesis and evaluation, reduce the potential for the score to be higher. Moreover, it remains to be seen whether the VC-Judge judge can generalize to other datasets, and more importantly other models that may require more sophisticated methods for LLM vulnerability detection.

- **Score**: 7/10

### RouteNator: A Router-Based Multi-Modal Architecture for Generating Synthetic Training Data for Function Calling LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10495v1)
- **Authors**: Vibha Belavadi, Tushar Vatsa, Dewang Sultania, Suhas Suresha, Ishita Verma, Cheng Chen, Tracy Holloway King, Michael Friedrich
- **Abstract**: This paper addresses fine-tuning Large Language Models (LLMs) for function calling tasks when real user interaction data is unavailable. In digital content creation tools, where users express their needs through natural language queries that must be mapped to API calls, the lack of real-world task-specific data and privacy constraints for training on it necessitate synthetic data generation. Existing approaches to synthetic data generation fall short in diversity and complexity, failing to replicate real-world data distributions and leading to suboptimal performance after LLM fine-tuning. We present a novel router-based architecture that leverages domain resources like content metadata and structured knowledge graphs, along with text-to-text and vision-to-text language models to generate high-quality synthetic training data. Our architecture's flexible routing mechanism enables synthetic data generation that matches observed real-world distributions, addressing a fundamental limitation of traditional approaches. Evaluation on a comprehensive set of real user queries demonstrates significant improvements in both function classification accuracy and API parameter selection. Models fine-tuned with our synthetic data consistently outperform traditional approaches, establishing new benchmarks for function calling tasks.
- **Summary**: Here is a concise summary and critical evaluation of the paper "RouteNator: A Router-Based Multi-Modal Architecture for Generating Synthetic Training Data for Function Calling LLMs":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of generating synthetic training data for function calling in Large Language Models (LLMs) where real user data is scarce or unavailable due to privacy concerns. They propose RouteNator, a novel router-based architecture that integrates domain resources like content metadata, knowledge graphs, text-to-text LLMs, and vision-to-text models to generate high-quality synthetic data. The architecture uses a weighted router to direct query generation requests to specialized prompt templates based on population-level statistics, ensuring realistic query patterns and coverage across different content categories.  The authors demonstrate significant improvements in function classification accuracy and API parameter selection compared to traditional synthetic data generation approaches.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of a router-based architecture for synthetic data generation for function calling is moderately novel. The integration of multiple modalities (text and vision) and the use of population-level statistics to guide routing enhance this novelty. However, the individual components (LLMs, Knowledge graphs) are not novel in themselves. The key novelty lies in the *combination* of these elements in a way tailored to the function calling data generation problem.

*   **Significance:** The problem addressed is significant, especially in the context of digital content creation platforms where user intent needs to be accurately translated into API calls, while avoiding the use of sensitive real user data. The empirical results demonstrate improvements over baseline methods and show the value of the proposed approach. However, the experiments are limited to a specific domain (digital content creation) and the generalizability of the approach to other domains is not explored. The lack of direct comparison against more recent and sophisticated synthetic data generation methods specifically designed for function calling (beyond Patil et al., 2023) is also a weakness.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The integration of multiple modalities (text and vision) to increase data diversity is a strength.
    *   The use of population-level statistics for routing provides a data-driven approach to generating realistic query patterns.
    *   The paper demonstrates tangible improvements in model performance on a real-world task.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The evaluation is primarily focused on one specific domain.
    *   There's limited discussion of the limitations of the generated data. For example, even with multi-modality, there's a risk of generating synthetic data with specific biases. The paper could benefit from a thorough analysis of biases.
    *   While the integration of metadata and KG is mentioned, the paper lacks a deeper dive into the types of KG used and how the metadata is preprocessed.
    *   The results, while showing improvement, aren’t overwhelmingly high. Achieving a function classification F1-score of 0.88 is good but leaves room for significant further enhancement, suggesting the method is not a complete solution.
    *   The novelty is more incremental than revolutionary.
    *   The paper relies on some domain-specific expertise, and it is unclear how this knowledge can be generalized to other domains.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of more effective synthetic data generation techniques for function calling, particularly in domains where user data is limited. The router-based architecture provides a flexible framework for incorporating different data sources and models.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a moderately novel and significant contribution to the field of synthetic data generation for function calling. It offers a practical architecture that leverages existing resources in a creative way to address a real-world problem. However, the limited scope of the evaluation, the incremental nature of the novelty, and the lack of detailed analysis of the data generated prevent it from achieving a higher score.

### S3C2 Summit 2024-09: Industry Secure Software Supply Chain Summit
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10538v1)
- **Authors**: Imranur Rahman, Yasemin Acar, Michel Cukier, William Enck, Christian Kastner, Alexandros Kapravelos, Dominik Wermke, Laurie Williams
- **Abstract**: While providing economic and software development value, software supply chains are only as strong as their weakest link. Over the past several years, there has been an exponential increase in cyberattacks, specifically targeting vulnerable links in critical software supply chains. These attacks disrupt the day-to-day functioning and threaten the security of nearly everyone on the internet, from billion-dollar companies and government agencies to hobbyist open-source developers. The ever-evolving threat of software supply chain attacks has garnered interest from the software industry and the US government in improving software supply chain security. On September 20, 2024, three researchers from the NSF-backed Secure Software Supply Chain Center (S3C2) conducted a Secure Software Supply Chain Summit with a diverse set of 12 practitioners from 9 companies. The goals of the Summit were to: (1) to enable sharing between individuals from different companies regarding practical experiences and challenges with software supply chain security, (2) to help form new collaborations, (3) to share our observations from our previous summits with industry, and (4) to learn about practitioners' challenges to inform our future research direction. The summit consisted of discussions of six topics relevant to the companies represented, including updating vulnerable dependencies, component and container choice, malicious commits, building infrastructure, large language models, and reducing entire classes of vulnerabilities.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper summarizes the discussions and key takeaways from an Industry Secure Software Supply Chain Summit held in September 2024. The summit, organized by researchers from the NSF-backed Secure Software Supply Chain Center (S3C2), brought together practitioners from various companies to discuss practical experiences and challenges in software supply chain security. The summit focused on six key topics: updating vulnerable dependencies, component and container choice, malicious commits, build infrastructure, large language models (LLMs), and reducing entire classes of vulnerabilities. The paper presents the current state of practice within the industry for each topic, highlighting challenges, ongoing efforts, and open questions. Key themes include the difficulties of managing dependencies, the evolving threat of malicious commits, the importance of secure build infrastructure, and the emerging challenges of securing LLMs in the software supply chain. The paper concludes by summarizing the main takeaways and acknowledging the summit participants and the National Science Foundation for their support.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in its timely report on the industry's perspective on software supply chain security challenges. While the individual topics discussed are not entirely novel, the collection of viewpoints from diverse industry practitioners provides a valuable snapshot of the current state of practice and the pain points experienced in the field. It surfaces the industry's specific difficulties that can be further researched and improved, making a significant impact to practitioners.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the paper stems from its ability to bridge the gap between academic research and industrial practice. By capturing the real-world experiences and challenges faced by practitioners, the paper can inform future research directions and facilitate the development of practical solutions that address the most pressing needs of the industry. The highlighting of open questions encourages further exploration and innovation in software supply chain security. Its coverage of nascent risks and challenges associated with the utilization of LLMs for supply chain security provides an early look at emerging issues.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Real-world perspective:** The paper provides valuable insights from industry practitioners.
    *   **Comprehensive coverage:**  The paper covers a wide range of relevant topics in software supply chain security.
    *   **Identifies key challenges:** The paper highlights specific challenges faced by practitioners.
    *   **Informs future research:** The open questions raised can guide future research efforts.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited depth:**  The paper summarizes discussions, offering more breadth than depth.
    *   **Chatham House Rule:** While preserving anonymity is crucial, it makes it harder to assess the credibility of claims.
    *   **Potential Bias:** The selection of participants may introduce bias, even though an intentional, diverse group was considered.
    *   **Lack of quantitative data:**  The findings are largely qualitative, lacking quantitative data to support claims or assess the prevalence of specific practices.

*   **Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the direction of research in software supply chain security and to inform the development of practical tools and techniques for improving security practices within the industry. By identifying key challenges and open questions, the paper can stimulate further investigation and innovation in this important area. The paper also serves as a valuable resource for practitioners seeking to understand the current state of practice and to identify potential areas for improvement within their own organizations.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper does not present groundbreaking research or introduce fundamentally new concepts, it provides a valuable service by capturing the collective experiences and perspectives of industry practitioners in a timely and relevant manner. Its emphasis on industry needs and challenges, along with the open questions raised, makes it an important contribution to the field. It makes the industry's challenges and concerns explicit. Thus, while the individual components may not be particularly groundbreaking, the paper's overall contribution warrants a score of 7.

### Exploring Implicit Visual Misunderstandings in Multimodal Large Language Models through Attention Analysis
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10541v1)
- **Authors**: Pengfei Wang, Guohai Xu, Weinong Wang, Junjie Yang, Jie Lou, Yunhua Xue
- **Abstract**: Recent advancements have enhanced the capability of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) to comprehend multi-image information. However, existing benchmarks primarily evaluate answer correctness, overlooking whether models genuinely comprehend the visual input. To address this, we define implicit visual misunderstanding (IVM), where MLLMs provide correct answers without fully comprehending the visual input. Through our analysis, we decouple the visual and textual modalities within the causal attention module, revealing that attention distribution increasingly converges on the image associated with the correct answer as the network layers deepen. This insight leads to the introduction of a scale-agnostic metric, \textit{attention accuracy}, and a novel benchmark for quantifying IVMs. Attention accuracy directly evaluates the model's visual understanding via internal mechanisms, remaining robust to positional biases for more reliable assessments. Furthermore, we extend our approach to finer granularities and demonstrate its effectiveness in unimodal scenarios, underscoring its versatility and generalizability.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces the concept of "implicit visual misunderstanding" (IVM) in Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs). IVM occurs when an MLLM provides a correct answer to a multi-image question, but doesn't fully understand the visual input; the model attends to the wrong image while still answering correctly. The authors analyze the attention mechanisms within MLLMs, decoupling visual and textual modalities. They find that as network layers deepen, attention increasingly focuses on the image associated with the correct answer. This leads to the definition of "attention accuracy" and the creation of a new benchmark dataset, "Single-Target Multimodal Evaluation" (STME), for quantifying IVMs.  The paper analyzes various MLLM architectures using STME, evaluating their ability to attend to the correct visual input. The authors also extend their approach to finer-grained token levels and demonstrate its effectiveness in unimodal scenarios.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces a valuable new perspective by shifting the focus from solely answer correctness to evaluating genuine visual understanding in MLLMs. Defining IVM is a significant contribution. Existing benchmarks primarily focus on whether the model gets the answer right, overlooking *how* the model arrives at the answer, especially when multiple images are involved. The concept of attention accuracy and the STME benchmark are new and directly address this gap. However, while the concept of IVM is novel, the *method* used to measure it (attention analysis) is not entirely new, as attention analysis has been used in NLP and vision before. The key novelty is in *how* this technique is applied and interpreted in the context of multi-image MLLMs.
*   **Significance:** Identifying and quantifying IVMs is important for improving the reliability and trustworthiness of MLLMs. MLLMs are increasingly being used in applications where visual input is critical (e.g., medical diagnosis, autonomous driving). A model that provides correct answers based on faulty visual processing is dangerous. The STME benchmark provides a valuable resource for future research in this area, enabling researchers to develop more robust and visually grounded MLLMs. The work directly addresses the limitations of existing multi-image benchmarks.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear Problem Definition: The paper articulates the problem of IVMs convincingly.
    *   Solid Methodology: Decoupling the attention mechanism and defining attention accuracy offers a reasonable way to quantify visual understanding. The STME benchmark is well-designed.
    *   Comprehensive Evaluation: The paper analyzes several MLLM architectures and explores the impact of factors such as model scale and task difficulty. The extension to token-level analysis is insightful.
    *   Generalizability: Applying the approach to unimodal scenarios shows the metric's versatility.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Indirect Measurement: Attention analysis provides an indirect measure of visual understanding. While the authors argue that attention focusing on the target image is a good proxy for understanding, it's not a perfect indicator. There might be other internal mechanisms that contribute to understanding that are not captured by attention analysis.
    *   Limited Scope: The focus is on single-target visual tasks. It is not clear how IVM manifests (or how to measure it) in more open-ended, complex visual reasoning scenarios.
    *   Computational Demands: Analyzing attention matrices can be computationally expensive, especially for large-scale models. This might limit the scalability of the proposed approach.
    *   Lack of mitigation strategies: The paper primarily focuses on identifying and quantifying IVMs, but it does not propose specific techniques for mitigating or eliminating them.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper could lead to a new line of research focused on developing more robust and visually grounded MLLMs.  It has the potential to influence the design of future benchmarks and evaluation metrics. However, the actual impact will depend on how widely the STME benchmark is adopted and how effective attention accuracy proves to be in driving improvements in MLLM architectures.

The work's value stems from identifying an important flaw in how we currently evaluate MLLMs: over-reliance on answer correctness. The paper offers a meaningful first step towards a more holistic assessment. But given attention mechanisms aren't perfect proxies and mitigation strategies are absent, the contribution is solid but not groundbreaking.

**Score: 7**

*Justification:* The paper presents a novel concept (IVM), introduces a reasonable methodology (attention analysis), and creates a valuable resource (STME). It demonstrates a clear understanding of the limitations of existing approaches and offers a valuable contribution to the field. The work also contains good empirical analyses with several MLLMs. The primary limitations are that it provides only an *indirect* measurement of visual understanding, its limited focus on a subset of all visual reasoning tasks, and its lack of specific mitigation strategies. The score reflects the paper's strong conceptual contribution and sound methodology, offset by the limitations mentioned above.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Towards a Deeper Understanding of Reasoning Capabilities in Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10543v1)
- **Authors**: Annie Wong, Thomas Bäck, Aske Plaat, Niki van Stein, Anna V. Kononova
- **Abstract**: While large language models demonstrate impressive performance on static benchmarks, the true potential of large language models as self-learning and reasoning agents in dynamic environments remains unclear. This study systematically evaluates the efficacy of self-reflection, heuristic mutation, and planning as prompting techniques to test the adaptive capabilities of agents. We conduct experiments with various open-source language models in dynamic environments and find that larger models generally outperform smaller ones, but that strategic prompting can close this performance gap. Second, a too-long prompt can negatively impact smaller models on basic reactive tasks, while larger models show more robust behaviour. Third, advanced prompting techniques primarily benefit smaller models on complex games, but offer less improvement for already high-performing large language models. Yet, we find that advanced reasoning methods yield highly variable outcomes: while capable of significantly improving performance when reasoning and decision-making align, they also introduce instability and can lead to big performance drops. Compared to human performance, our findings reveal little evidence of true emergent reasoning. Instead, large language model performance exhibits persistent limitations in crucial areas such as planning, reasoning, and spatial coordination, suggesting that current-generation large language models still suffer fundamental shortcomings that may not be fully overcome through self-reflective prompting alone. Reasoning is a multi-faceted task, and while reasoning methods like Chain of thought improves multi-step reasoning on math word problems, our findings using dynamic benchmarks highlight important shortcomings in general reasoning capabilities, indicating a need to move beyond static benchmarks to capture the complexity of reasoning.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Towards a Deeper Understanding of Reasoning Capabilities in Large Language Models" investigates the ability of Large Language Models (LLMs) to learn and adapt in dynamic environments using prompting techniques. It systematically compares several open-source LLMs on dynamic decision-making tasks within the SMARTPLAY benchmark. The prompting strategies explored include self-reflection, heuristic mutation, and planning. Key findings include: larger models generally perform better, but strategic prompting can partially close the performance gap; excessively long prompts can negatively impact smaller models; advanced prompting techniques primarily benefit smaller models; and advanced reasoning methods lead to highly variable outcomes. The authors also find limited evidence of true emergent reasoning, suggesting LLMs have limitations in planning, reasoning, and spatial coordination. A denser, task-aligned reward system can improve learning effectiveness.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its systematic comparison of multiple open-source LLMs on a comprehensive set of dynamic decision-making tasks (SMARTPLAY). While the individual prompting techniques (self-reflection, planning, heuristic mutation) are not entirely novel, the paper offers a comparative analysis of their efficacy across different models and environments. The insight that excessive reasoning and longer prompts can hurt smaller models is a contribution, as is the demonstration of the variable performance gains yielded from advanced reasoning strategies. Transforming the reward function from sparse to dense to reduce manual prompt engineering is insightful.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial question: how well can LLM agents autonomously learn and adapt in dynamic environments, going beyond static benchmark performance? The work highlights the persistent limitations of current LLMs in reasoning and planning, despite their impressive capabilities on static tasks. This is significant because it tempers the hype surrounding emergent reasoning and pinpoints areas where LLMs still struggle, providing valuable insights for future research. The demonstration that well-designed prompts can make smaller models competitive and more stable than larger models is significant.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper systematically tests a range of prompting strategies across different LLMs and environments.
    *   **Practical Insights:**  It identifies conditions where prompting fails and offers actionable advice (e.g., using dense reward systems).
    *   **Empirical Evidence:** The findings are based on extensive experiments.
    *   **Clear Research Question:** The study is focused on a specific, relevant problem.
    *   **Well-Written:** The paper is clearly written and well-organized.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Exploration of Architectural Impacts:** While the paper investigates model size, it provides limited exploration of how different LLM architectures influence reasoning capabilities.
    *   **Reliance on Open-Source LLMs:** Although understandable due to resource constraints, restricting the analysis to open-source LLMs means the study may not fully reflect the capabilities of state-of-the-art closed-source models.
    *   **Limited Task Complexity:** While SMARTPLAY is a good benchmark, the tasks are not as complex as real-world scenarios. Results may not extrapolate as directly.
    *   **Variability in Results:** The high variance observed in some experiments weakens the conclusions and indicates potential instability in LLM behavior. The authors acknowledge this, but a deeper analysis of the causes of this variability would improve the paper.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's findings could influence future research directions by encouraging the development of more robust prompting strategies, more comprehensive evaluation benchmarks, and a deeper understanding of the limitations of LLMs in dynamic settings. It also emphasizes the need to move beyond static benchmarks when evaluating LLM capabilities.

*   **Rigorous Rationale:** The novelty is moderate, and significance is relatively high due to the comparative nature of the study, its demonstration of failure modes, and its insights into improving LLM performance in dynamic environments. The weaknesses slightly temper the overall impact.
Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Pharmacophore-Conditioned Diffusion Model for Ligand-Based De Novo Drug Design
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10545v1)
- **Authors**: Amira Alakhdar, Barnabas Poczos, Newell Washburn
- **Abstract**: Developing bioactive molecules remains a central, time- and cost-heavy challenge in drug discovery, particularly for novel targets lacking structural or functional data. Pharmacophore modeling presents an alternative for capturing the key features required for molecular bioactivity against a biological target. In this work, we present PharmaDiff, a pharmacophore-conditioned diffusion model for 3D molecular generation. PharmaDiff employs a transformer-based architecture to integrate an atom-based representation of the 3D pharmacophore into the generative process, enabling the precise generation of 3D molecular graphs that align with predefined pharmacophore hypotheses. Through comprehensive testing, PharmaDiff demonstrates superior performance in matching 3D pharmacophore constraints compared to ligand-based drug design methods. Additionally, it achieves higher docking scores across a range of proteins in structure-based drug design, without the need for target protein structures. By integrating pharmacophore modeling with 3D generative techniques, PharmaDiff offers a powerful and flexible framework for rational drug design.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've reviewed the paper "Pharmacophore-Conditioned Diffusion Model for Ligand-Based De Novo Drug Design". Here's a summary, followed by a critical evaluation and score:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces PharmaDiff, a novel generative model that leverages diffusion models and 3D pharmacophore modeling for de novo drug design. PharmaDiff generates 3D molecular structures conditioned on an atom-based representation of 3D pharmacophore hypotheses. The architecture uses a transformer-based approach to integrate pharmacophore information into the generative process, allowing for the precise generation of molecules that align with predefined pharmacophore constraints.  The model is evaluated using both ligand-based and structure-based drug design scenarios.  Results indicate that PharmaDiff outperforms existing methods in matching 3D pharmacophore constraints and achieving higher docking scores against target proteins without the target protein structure, suggesting a promising framework for rational drug design.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Novelty:** The key strength lies in explicitly incorporating 3D pharmacophore information into a diffusion model for *de novo* molecular generation. While previous methods used pharmacophores, PharmaDiff's atom-based conditioning within the diffusion framework appears to be a genuine advancement. It uses a mixed categorical and gaussian diffusion model which is state of the art.
    *   **Performance:** The experimental results are compelling. The model demonstrates superior performance in matching 3D pharmacophore constraints compared to SMILES-based methods like PGMG and similarity-based methods like REINVENT4. The improved docking scores without target protein structure are significant.
    *   **Thorough Evaluation:** The authors present a fairly comprehensive evaluation, covering various metrics including validity, uniqueness, novelty, diversity, pharmacophore matching scores, and drug-likeness properties. The comparison to relevant baselines strengthens the claims.
    *   **Structure-based Design Demonstration:** The evaluation on the structure-based drug design scenario is a strong point. Being able to generate molecules compatible with protein binding sites *without* explicitly using the target protein structure during training is a valuable capability.
    *   **Implementation details:** The authors clearly described their implementation of the inpainting method and the cross attention layer.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Inpainting issues:** The authors acknowledge the potential issue of losing key pharmacophoric features during the inpainting process. Although they mention implicit incorporation as contextual input, this potential drawback is a genuine limitations.
    *   **Connectivity Issues:** Inpainting is known to lead to connectivity issues, which is noted by the authors. Further research is needed to strengthen the molecular graph.
    *   **Computational Cost:** While not explicitly discussed, diffusion models are generally computationally expensive to train and sample from. The paper lacks details about the computational resources needed and the generation time, which could limit its practicality in some settings.
    *   **Limited Exploration of Chemical Space:** While the authors show good performance on drug-likeness, the chemical diversity is only slightly improved compared to the DiffSBDD baseline. Further exploring the chemical space would be beneficial.
    *   **Baseline Limitations:**  The baselines, while relevant, are not always the most recent or advanced approaches. Comparing against other state-of-the-art generative models that incorporate some form of structural conditioning would provide a stronger comparison.
    *   **Hyperparameter tuning:** The authors mentioned that they selected the hyperparameters of the loss function based on a "series of short numerical experiments." Those hyperparameters could be explored more thoroughly.

*   **Significance and Impact:**

    *   PharmaDiff has the potential to accelerate and improve the rational design of drugs, especially for targets where structural information is limited. By combining pharmacophore modeling with deep generative techniques, the model allows for a more precise and efficient exploration of the chemical space.
    *   It could also be a valuable tool for lead optimization, enabling the generation of molecules with improved binding affinity and selectivity.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a solid contribution to the field of *de novo* drug design. The explicit integration of 3D pharmacophore information into a diffusion model is novel, and the results demonstrate improved performance in terms of pharmacophore matching and docking scores. However, the limitations related to computational cost, baseline comparisons, the inpainting process and loss functions, and hyperparameter choices prevent a higher score. While the model shows promise, further development is needed to address these weaknesses and fully realize its potential.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Does Feasibility Matter? Understanding the Impact of Feasibility on Synthetic Training Data
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10551v1)
- **Authors**: Yiwen Liu, Jessica Bader, Jae Myung Kim
- **Abstract**: With the development of photorealistic diffusion models, models trained in part or fully on synthetic data achieve progressively better results. However, diffusion models still routinely generate images that would not exist in reality, such as a dog floating above the ground or with unrealistic texture artifacts. We define the concept of feasibility as whether attributes in a synthetic image could realistically exist in the real-world domain; synthetic images containing attributes that violate this criterion are considered infeasible. Intuitively, infeasible images are typically considered out-of-distribution; thus, training on such images is expected to hinder a model's ability to generalize to real-world data, and they should therefore be excluded from the training set whenever possible. However, does feasibility really matter? In this paper, we investigate whether enforcing feasibility is necessary when generating synthetic training data for CLIP-based classifiers, focusing on three target attributes: background, color, and texture. We introduce VariReal, a pipeline that minimally edits a given source image to include feasible or infeasible attributes given by the textual prompt generated by a large language model. Our experiments show that feasibility minimally affects LoRA-fine-tuned CLIP performance, with mostly less than 0.3% difference in top-1 accuracy across three fine-grained datasets. Also, the attribute matters on whether the feasible/infeasible images adversarially influence the classification performance. Finally, mixing feasible and infeasible images in training datasets does not significantly impact performance compared to using purely feasible or infeasible datasets.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper, "Understanding the Impact of Feasibility on Synthetic Training Data":

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the impact of "feasibility" (whether synthetic image attributes could realistically exist in the real world) on the performance of CLIP-based classifiers trained with synthetic data.  The authors introduce VariReal, a pipeline for minimally editing real images to generate both feasible and infeasible image variations across three attributes: background, color, and texture. They fine-tune CLIP classifiers using these synthetic datasets (alone and in combination with real data) and then assess their performance on fine-grained classification tasks. The main findings are counter-intuitive: feasibility doesn't significantly affect classification performance, and background modifications are consistently beneficial while color and texture changes have limited or even detrimental effects.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core concept of rigorously examining feasibility in synthetic training data is a valuable contribution. While prior work has acknowledged the existence of unrealistic images generated by diffusion models, this paper directly tackles the question of whether enforcing feasibility is necessary. VariReal, the pipeline, while relying on established techniques (Stable Diffusion, ControlNet), is carefully designed for this specific research question. The specific findings about the limited benefit of feasibility and the contrasting impact of background versus foreground manipulations are also novel.

* **Significance:** The paper challenges the common assumption that synthetic data must be highly realistic (i.e., feasible) to be useful. The implication is that for certain tasks, particularly with CLIP models, a broader range of synthetic data, even with some degree of infeasibility, can be effective. This could potentially simplify synthetic data generation pipelines, reducing the computational cost of enforcing strict realism.  The results suggest that carefully choosing the *attributes* to modify in synthetic data generation is more important than ensuring every generated image is perfectly realistic.

* **Strengths:**
    *   **Well-defined Experiment:** The study is rigorously designed with clear definitions, controls, and evaluation metrics. The use of minimally edited images generated from a real image foundation helps isolate the impact of the manipulated features.
    *   **Systematic Analysis:** The analysis is thorough, considering multiple datasets, fine-tuning strategies, and evaluation metrics (accuracy, FID, CLIP score, etc.). The investigation of inclusion and Jaccard indices to assess the diversity of learned information is insightful.
    *   **Counter-Intuitive Findings:** The findings challenge conventional wisdom and open new avenues for research into the use of synthetic data.
    *   **Strong Ablation and User Studies:** Qualitative results are complemented with user studies and ablation experiments providing additional support for the authors' claims.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scope:** The study focuses only on CLIP-based classifiers and three specific image attributes. It's unclear how well these findings generalize to other models, architectures, or attributes (e.g., shape, lighting).
    *   **Reliance on Existing Techniques:**  VariReal relies on existing diffusion models and editing methods, which have their own limitations and biases. As diffusion models improve, the quality and characteristics of infeasible images will change, possibly affecting the conclusions.
    *   **Definition of Feasibility:** The definition of "feasibility" is subjective and could be further refined. For example, focusing solely on "official manufacturer colors" ignores the vast landscape of real-world modifications and customization that people engage in.
    *   **Possible Bias in GPT-4 Generation of Prompts:** Though GPT-4 is useful for its generality, prompts from a single LLM could be potentially narrow in variety and possibly influence the classification/training in unexpected ways.

* **Potential Influence:**  The paper encourages a more nuanced approach to synthetic data generation, suggesting that focusing on modifying specific *relevant* attributes is more crucial than blanket realism. This could shift research towards developing more targeted and efficient synthetic data generation strategies. The work could also inspire further investigations into the benefits of "controlled infeasibility" for regularizing models and improving robustness.

* **Score:** 7.5

**Justification:** The paper presents a novel and well-executed study with counter-intuitive findings that are likely to influence the field of synthetic data generation for computer vision. While the scope is limited to specific model architectures and image attributes, the study's rigor and the potential impact of its conclusions warrant a high score. It presents interesting findings that challenge prior conventions and may drive future innovation in synthetic data generation pipelines. Further work will be required to determine the breadth of impact on other domains.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Beyond 'Aha!': Toward Systematic Meta-Abilities Alignment in Large Reasoning Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10554v1)
- **Authors**: Zhiyuan Hu, Yibo Wang, Hanze Dong, Yuhui Xu, Amrita Saha, Caiming Xiong, Bryan Hooi, Junnan Li
- **Abstract**: Large reasoning models (LRMs) already possess a latent capacity for long chain-of-thought reasoning. Prior work has shown that outcome-based reinforcement learning (RL) can incidentally elicit advanced reasoning behaviors such as self-correction, backtracking, and verification phenomena often referred to as the model's "aha moment". However, the timing and consistency of these emergent behaviors remain unpredictable and uncontrollable, limiting the scalability and reliability of LRMs' reasoning capabilities. To address these limitations, we move beyond reliance on prompts and coincidental "aha moments". Instead, we explicitly align models with three meta-abilities: deduction, induction, and abduction, using automatically generated, self-verifiable tasks. Our three stage-pipeline individual alignment, parameter-space merging, and domain-specific reinforcement learning, boosting performance by over 10\% relative to instruction-tuned baselines. Furthermore, domain-specific RL from the aligned checkpoint yields an additional 2\% average gain in the performance ceiling across math, coding, and science benchmarks, demonstrating that explicit meta-ability alignment offers a scalable and dependable foundation for reasoning. Code is available at: https://github.com/zhiyuanhubj/Meta-Ability-Alignment
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Beyond 'Aha!’: Toward Systematic Meta-Abilities Alignment in Large Reasoning Models" addresses the issue of unpredictable and uncontrollable reasoning behaviors (the "aha moment") in large language models (LLMs).  Instead of relying on emergent behaviors during reinforcement learning (RL), the authors propose explicitly aligning LLMs with three core reasoning meta-abilities: deduction, induction, and abduction. They create a task suite with programmatically generated, self-verifiable tasks for each meta-ability and use a three-stage pipeline: 1) individual alignment to each meta-ability, 2) parameter-space merging of the aligned models, and 3) domain-specific RL fine-tuning.  Experiments on math, coding, and science benchmarks demonstrate improved performance compared to instruction-tuned baselines and show that meta-ability alignment raises the performance ceiling for subsequent domain-specific RL.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its **systematic approach** to aligning LLMs with specific, well-defined meta-reasoning abilities (deduction, induction, abduction) derived from Peirce's inference triad.  While prior work explored emergent reasoning through RL, this paper provides a structured method to *engineer* these abilities. The automatically generated, self-verifiable tasks for each meta-ability are a valuable contribution, particularly for out-of-distribution training. Parameter-space merging and subsequent domain-specific RL isn't completely new, but the way it's applied here, following meta-ability alignment, is a novel aspect.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from the **potential to improve the reliability and controllability of LLM reasoning**.  By moving beyond emergent behaviors, the paper offers a path to building more robust and predictable reasoning systems.  The reported performance gains across multiple benchmarks (math, coding, science) suggest that the aligned meta-abilities generalize well.  The finding that meta-ability alignment boosts the performance ceiling for downstream RL is also important, indicating a stronger foundation for learning complex reasoning tasks. The paper also offers an automated framework for generating tasks aligned with meta reasoning.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:**  The paper clearly identifies the limitations of relying on emergent reasoning.
    *   **Well-Defined Methodology:** The proposed three-stage pipeline is well-structured and easy to understand.
    *   **Comprehensive Experiments:**  The experiments cover multiple benchmarks and model sizes (7B and 32B), providing strong evidence for the effectiveness of the approach.
    *   **Reproducibility:** The code is released, allowing for verification and further research.
    *   **Strong Results:** The performance gains compared to strong instruction-tuned baselines are compelling.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scope of Meta-Abilities:** While deduction, induction, and abduction are important, they don't represent the *entirety* of human reasoning.  Other meta-reasoning skills (e.g., analogical reasoning, causal inference) could be explored.
    *   **Reliance on Synthetic Data:** The meta-ability alignment relies on synthetic tasks. While this ensures control and verifiability, it might not fully capture the complexities of real-world reasoning.
    *   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** The paper mentions that hyperparameters for merging and RL are chosen empirically. It would be helpful to see analysis on the sensitivity of results on hyperparameters.
    *   **Modest Gains in RL fine-tuning:** While the gains by RL fine-tuning from meta aligned models is present, it isn't drastic. This could indicate there is a diminishing return effect from explicit meta reasoning training.
    *   **Lack of Ablation Studies:** Lack of ablation studies on how much each alignment contributes.

*   **Potential Impact:**  The paper has the potential to influence the development of more reliable and controllable LLMs.  The meta-ability alignment approach could be integrated into existing training pipelines and extended to other reasoning skills.  The task suite could be used to benchmark and evaluate LLM reasoning capabilities. It lays out a principled manner of building specific capabilities instead of relying on emergent abilities, which would bring more reliability.

**Score:** 7.5

**Rationale:** The paper presents a novel and systematic approach to improving LLM reasoning by explicitly aligning models with core meta-abilities. The performance gains are significant, and the code release promotes reproducibility. However, the limitations of relying on synthetic data and the limited scope of meta-abilities prevent it from receiving a higher score. It's a valuable contribution but requires further exploration and refinement. The increase in RL is marginal compared to the instruction tuned model, which brings into question whether this method is the most optimal to build reasoning skills.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### End-to-End Vision Tokenizer Tuning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10562v1)
- **Authors**: Wenxuan Wang, Fan Zhang, Yufeng Cui, Haiwen Diao, Zhuoyan Luo, Huchuan Lu, Jing Liu, Xinlong Wang
- **Abstract**: Existing vision tokenization isolates the optimization of vision tokenizers from downstream training, implicitly assuming the visual tokens can generalize well across various tasks, e.g., image generation and visual question answering. The vision tokenizer optimized for low-level reconstruction is agnostic to downstream tasks requiring varied representations and semantics. This decoupled paradigm introduces a critical misalignment: The loss of the vision tokenization can be the representation bottleneck for target tasks. For example, errors in tokenizing text in a given image lead to poor results when recognizing or generating them. To address this, we propose ETT, an end-to-end vision tokenizer tuning approach that enables joint optimization between vision tokenization and target autoregressive tasks. Unlike prior autoregressive models that use only discrete indices from a frozen vision tokenizer, ETT leverages the visual embeddings of the tokenizer codebook, and optimizes the vision tokenizers end-to-end with both reconstruction and caption objectives. ETT can be seamlessly integrated into existing training pipelines with minimal architecture modifications. Our ETT is simple to implement and integrate, without the need to adjust the original codebooks or architectures of the employed large language models. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our proposed end-to-end vision tokenizer tuning unlocks significant performance gains, i.e., 2-6% for multimodal understanding and visual generation tasks compared to frozen tokenizer baselines, while preserving the original reconstruction capability. We hope this very simple and strong method can empower multimodal foundation models besides image generation and understanding.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the issue of vision tokenizer optimization being isolated from downstream tasks in multimodal learning. It argues that the standard practice of training vision tokenizers for low-level reconstruction (e.g., pixel reconstruction) leads to a misalignment with downstream tasks requiring higher-level semantic representations. To tackle this, the authors propose an End-to-End Vision Tokenizer Tuning (ETT) approach.  ETT enables joint optimization of the vision tokenizer and target autoregressive tasks. It leverages the codebook embeddings of the tokenizer rather than relying solely on discrete indices and incorporates a token-level caption loss during training.  The method is designed to be easily integrated into existing architectures without modifying the large language model (LLM).  Experiments show performance gains in multimodal understanding and visual generation tasks compared to frozen tokenizer baselines while preserving the tokenizer's reconstruction capability.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of end-to-end tuning of a vision tokenizer is reasonably novel. The standard paradigm has indeed been to train them separately. While vision-language models often fine-tune continuous vision encoders, end-to-end fine-tuning of *discrete* vision tokenizers is less common.  The use of codebook embeddings rather than just indices is a good idea to preserve more information and enable gradient propagation, increasing novelty.

*   **Significance:** The potential significance lies in bridging the gap between low-level visual representation learning and higher-level multimodal tasks. If the proposed ETT method consistently demonstrates substantial improvements, it could become a standard practice in training multimodal models. The fact that the method is designed to be simple to implement and integrate with existing LLMs is another significant strength, facilitating wider adoption. The reported gains (2-6%) while keeping reconstruction relatively constant, highlight a trade-off but also a potentially better alignment between the tokenizer and downstream tasks.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:**  The paper clearly articulates the problem of misalignment between tokenizer training and downstream tasks.
    *   **Simple and Effective Approach:** ETT is conceptually simple and easy to implement. This "plug-and-play" nature is a major selling point.
    *   **Strong Experimental Results:** The experiments demonstrate consistent performance gains across a range of multimodal understanding and generation tasks.
    *   **Preservation of Reconstruction Capability:** The method is designed to preserve the original reconstruction capability of the tokenizer, addressing a potential concern that fine-tuning might degrade its fundamental image representation ability.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The ablation studies provide insights into the contribution of each component of the method.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental Improvement:** While the performance gains are consistent, they are in the 2-6% range. This is respectable, but not groundbreaking.
    *   **Limited Scope:**  The evaluation focuses on a specific set of tasks and benchmarks. It's unclear how well ETT would generalize to other modalities (e.g., audio, video) or tasks not explicitly considered.
    *   **Dependency on Existing Tokenizers:** The method refines existing vision tokenizers rather than learning from scratch. While this makes integration easier, it also limits the potential for radical improvements.  The paper also uses one specific tokenization method (IBQ), it would be important to establish whether ETT is consistently effective across different types of tokenizers.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper has the potential to influence the field by highlighting the importance of joint optimization in multimodal learning. The simplicity of ETT could encourage researchers and practitioners to adopt this approach as a standard practice. This could lead to more effective vision tokenizers and improved performance on a wider range of multimodal tasks.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a reasonably novel and effective approach to address an important problem in multimodal learning. The results are convincing, and the method is designed to be practical. However, the performance gains are incremental rather than revolutionary, and the scope of the evaluation is somewhat limited. The dependency of the method on pre-trained tokenizers also restricts the level of possible improvement.

Given these considerations, a score of 7 is appropriate. The paper presents a solid contribution with the potential to impact the field, but it lacks the groundbreaking nature to warrant a higher score.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### 3D-Fixup: Advancing Photo Editing with 3D Priors
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10566v1)
- **Authors**: Yen-Chi Cheng, Krishna Kumar Singh, Jae Shin Yoon, Alex Schwing, Liangyan Gui, Matheus Gadelha, Paul Guerrero, Nanxuan Zhao
- **Abstract**: Despite significant advances in modeling image priors via diffusion models, 3D-aware image editing remains challenging, in part because the object is only specified via a single image. To tackle this challenge, we propose 3D-Fixup, a new framework for editing 2D images guided by learned 3D priors. The framework supports difficult editing situations such as object translation and 3D rotation. To achieve this, we leverage a training-based approach that harnesses the generative power of diffusion models. As video data naturally encodes real-world physical dynamics, we turn to video data for generating training data pairs, i.e., a source and a target frame. Rather than relying solely on a single trained model to infer transformations between source and target frames, we incorporate 3D guidance from an Image-to-3D model, which bridges this challenging task by explicitly projecting 2D information into 3D space. We design a data generation pipeline to ensure high-quality 3D guidance throughout training. Results show that by integrating these 3D priors, 3D-Fixup effectively supports complex, identity coherent 3D-aware edits, achieving high-quality results and advancing the application of diffusion models in realistic image manipulation. The code is provided at https://3dfixup.github.io/
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "3D-Fixup: Advancing Photo Editing with 3D Priors":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces 3D-Fixup, a novel framework for 3D-aware photo editing. It aims to address limitations of existing methods, which either suffer from long inference times (optimization-based approaches) or lack the generality due to reliance on synthetic data and limited 3D reasoning (feed-forward approaches). 3D-Fixup uses a feed-forward model trained on a new dataset generated from real-world videos and enriched with 3D priors.  The core idea is to leverage 3D transformations estimated from video frames, combined with 3D reconstructions from an image-to-3D model, to provide structured 3D guidance during training.  This allows the model to perform complex, identity-preserving 3D edits like rotations and translations in natural images, with fine-grained user control and fast inference.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper has several novel aspects:
    * **Data Generation Pipeline:** The automated pipeline for creating 3D-aware image editing datasets from videos is a significant contribution.  The method of extracting 3D transformations from video frames and combining them with 3D reconstructions to generate training data is a clever way to circumvent the need for manual 3D annotations.
    * **3D Guidance:** The incorporation of 3D priors in the form of image-to-3D reconstructions to guide the image editing process is well-justified and effective, enabling fine-grained control of 3D manipulations.
    * **Model Architecture:** While the architecture is based on a pre-existing conditional diffusion model (MagicFixup), the authors' modifications to use the 3D guidance and focus on high-quality 3D edits make it a novel and valuable application.
    * **Fine-grained edits:** The ability to allow the user to have fine-grained 3D control of the object being edited is a welcomed departure from text-prompt edits that lack granularity.

* **Significance:** The paper has the potential to significantly impact the field of image editing.
    * **Improved Realism:** The experiments demonstrate that 3D-Fixup achieves more realistic and identity-preserving results compared to state-of-the-art methods, especially for large 3D transformations.
    * **Practical Applicability:** The feed-forward nature of the model allows for fast inference, making it suitable for real-world applications.  The demonstration of continuous rotations highlights the model's robustness and potential for interactive editing workflows.
    * **Bridging 2D/3D Gap:**  The work effectively bridges the gap between 2D and 3D image editing, making 3D manipulation accessible without requiring explicit 3D modeling expertise.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Strong Empirical Results:**  The paper provides extensive qualitative and quantitative evaluations, demonstrating the superiority of 3D-Fixup over various baselines.
    * **Well-Defined Problem:** The problem of 3D-aware image editing is clearly defined and well-motivated.
    * **Clear and Concise Writing:**  The paper is well-written and easy to follow.
    * **Open Source Code:** The availability of the code contributes to the reproducibility and further development of the method.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Dependence on Image-to-3D Quality:** The performance of 3D-Fixup is limited by the quality of the image-to-3D reconstructions. The paper acknowledges this limitation, noting that the model may struggle with occlusions and incomplete masks. Future research can improve these reconstruction methods.
    * **Detail Preservation:** The paper admits that some intricate details are not preserved well.
    * **Limited Scene Complexity:** The framework focuses mainly on single-object edits. Handling more complex scenes with multiple interacting objects remains a challenge.
    * **Lack of Failure Cases:** A section showing failure cases would enhance the paper's completeness and provide insights into the model's limitations.

* **Potential Influence:** 3D-Fixup has the potential to influence future research in image editing, particularly in the areas of 3D-aware manipulation, data generation, and diffusion model applications. It also could see use in applications in e-commerce or digital media.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of image editing by proposing a novel framework for 3D-aware manipulation. The automated data generation pipeline, the use of 3D priors, and the strong empirical results justify a high score.  While there are limitations, the authors acknowledge them and suggest directions for future research.  The combination of innovation, practical applicability, and potential influence warrant a high score.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10
